CIIINA TEAS.

Tea a hi Sir Andrew Clarke. For years, whenever my Customers have asked me for a Mild Tea, I have advised the Pure. old Fashioned China which I keep. and the advice has been appreciated. It is very satisfactory to them that such an eminent authority as Sir Andrew Clarke, the London Physician, corroborates opinion. Ilere is an extract from an interesting lecture, delivered by Sir Andrew the other day, to the Students of the London Hospital : "Tea is a blessed beverage. Ido not know what I should do without it. But there is tea and tea' an.l one of the teas I have in my mind is the representation of all that is physiologically wicked. Igo about town a good deal holding consulta- tions here and there, and about five o'clock, when I get into a place. the lady of the house will say to me : Sir Andrew, you '- tired, do let me give you EL CUP ot tea.' I say ' thank you very much. But the tea has stood for half-an-hour, and she - ' I know you do you not like it strong, Sir Andrew. and she then pours about a tablespoonful of the tea into a cup -)cup up with water. Now I call it positivecruelty to give tea like that to anybody and I hope you, gentlemen, set your face against it. Tea, to be useful, should be first of an Bleak China Tea the Indian Tea which is ivated has become BO POWerttli in its effects upon the nervous system that a cup of it taken early in the many do, so disorders the nervous system that those who take it actually get into a state of tea intoxication, and produces a form of nervous disturbance, which is most painful to witness, It you want to have, either for yourselves or patients, tea which will not injure and which will refresh, get Black China Tea, putting in the right measure—the 'oned teaspoonful for each person and one for the blessed pot. Then pour en briskly boiling water, and within five minutes you must pour it off again. or it will become wicked instead of good." Such is Sir Andrew Clarke's opinion and advise. A few words of warning, however, are necessary to those who have for- gotten what this old fashioned tea is like. The colour of the tea after it is brewed will be found fobe much paler than that now in use, and the taste may not be acceptable at first owing to its apparent insipidness ; but if its use is persisted in, it will very soon be found to be much more suitable for the system, and if, in a week to two after its use, a trial is again made of the kind now in ordinary use, you will wonder however you could have been using it,and you will endorse Sir Andrew's statement. This 0141 fashioned China Tea, as recommended by Sir Andrew Clarke, can be obtained now, as always or the last 35 years, at

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

lIERALDIC.—How many sleeves has a coat of arms " WOOD is the thing, after all," as the man with a wooden leg said when the mad dog bit it. ',Vnax scent would you name when accusing an Irishman of an untruth? —Patchouli (Pat, you lie). CONFLICTING CONCLUSIONS. -- Naturalists class the cat as a feline auhrial, whereas mice regard it as a most unfeline critter. THE cat-o'-nine-tails must have nine times as many lives as tails, or it could not have lasted so long. AN impatient youth at a weddisg party the other day, after an awful silence, suddenly exclaimed, "Don't be so unspeakably happy." IT is a fact that a pot of porter fetched by a juvenile becomes shorter the longer he has to carry it. THE reason why racing is generally called "the turf," must be owing to the fact of so many green blades being found upon ft.—Poach. A MAN advertises for "a competent person to undertake the sale of a new medicine ;" and adds that "it will be found profitable to the undertaker." No doubt of it.

A HATTER in Washington has invented a hat that cannot be blown oil by a gale. The editor of one of the 'papers of that city says, "If this hat were not windproof, we would give it a puff." Are old gentleman hearing his youthful nephew, a little boy who is versed in geology, talking about the "earths crust," inquired whether he was speaking of the outside of a dirt-pie ?—Punch. "YOU'VE destroyed my peace of mind, Betsy," said a desponding lover to a truant lass. "It can't do you much harm, John, for 'twas an amazing small piece you had, anyway," was the quick reply. "PAT, do you know how to drive ?" asked an excursionist in Ireland of the keeper of a jaunting car. "Sure do," was the answer; "wasn't it I who upset your honour in a ditch two years ago ?" WHEN we remember that ink is composed of gall and acid, we should no longer wonder at the quantity of ill-natured articles which flow from even the best-natured pens. A Moamar, gentleman while performing, was lately arrested by two bailiffs, who requested him to join them in a trio. "I should rather imagine," eaid the unfortunate gentleman, "you wish for a :etch !" A RAW Scotchman, who had evidently not been 1 long from "Aberdeen awa' " went into a shop in the city the other day, and inquired, " Whilk is the way to the Minoaries?"—" They speak Dutch , next door," was the immediate reply. A MARRIED man informs us that he knows a mode of conveying information much more rapidly than by the electric telegraph. He says he has nothing to do but to make a secret of information, and then tell it to his wife. A GENTLEMAN crossing Oxford-street, last week, was surprised on finding a sweeper at each end of the crossing, and, accosting one of them he said, "Hallo! What two sweepers to one crossing 7" to which the boy archly replied, "Yes, sir, one catemes them as t'other misses." "PAPA, I've been seeing cook make bread ; and can you tell me why dough resembles the sun 7"— " The sun, Freddy?"—" Yes, Pa."—" No, I can- not."—Freddy, with great glee, "Because when it rises, it is light." Pa, soliloquising, "That child is too clever to live." YOUTH is a glorious invention. While the girls chase the hours, and you chase the girls, the months seem to dance away "with down upon their feet." What a pity summer is so short ; before you know it, lovers become deacons, and romps grandmothers. LIBERALITY dies with pos ession ; the lover cries never mind the expense where his angel is con- cerned, but once let him get her, and he thinks so no more. The two shillings, that he used to con- sider a mere bagatelle, become in his imagination a cart-wheel. A STRANGER saw an Irishman leaning against a post. and watching a procession slowly issuing from an opposite house, when the following dialogue ensued. "Is that a funeral ?"—" Yes, sir, I'm thinkin' so."--" Any one of distinction ?" " Who is it?"—" Sure it's the jintleman in the coffin." A CELi DILATED Wolverhampton fiddler (now deceased) ran his gig against a broad-wheeled waggon, and upset himself in the dirt, on which he got up and stammered out to the driver, "I bei pardon, sir; I hope I have not done any damage. —" Lor bless you, no, sir," said the waggoner, "so as you hannat hurt yourscn." THE best part of a race, after all, is the cham- pagne and the eating and drinking. If you are betting, you cannot enjoy your "gi tab." You are thinking of the stakes, and how much you may have to fork out—instead of being intent, as you should be, upon forking out the stakes which are at the bottom of the pigeon-pie.--Punch. Two widowers were once condoling together on the deaths of their wives • one of them exclaimed, with a sigh, "Well may I bewail my loss, for I had SO few differences with the dear deceased that the last day of my marriage was as happy as the first." There I surpass you," said his friend, "for the last day of mine was happier." DURING a recent fire, an old woman was very anxious to go through a street, which was con- sidered at the time "dangerous," but all her efforts were unavailing. At length she pushed one of the policemen aside, when that worthy preserver of the public peace said, "Now, marm, you can't pass ; if you do, you'll be killed, and then you'll blame us afterwards." Discs on a time, a Frenchman and a Dutchman were travelling in Pennsylvania, when the horse lost a shoe. They drove up to a blacksmith's shop, and no one being in, they proceeded to the house to Inquire. The Frenchman rapped, and called out, "is de smitty wittiur —"Shtand back," say. Hans; "let me shpeak. Ish der blacksmith s shop en der house V' A DISTINCTION.—Napoleon was one day search- ing for a book in the library of Malmaison, and at last discovered it on a shelf somewhat above his reach. Marshal Moncey, who was present, one of the tallest men in the army, stepped forward, say- ing—" Permit me, sire; I am higher than your majesty."—" You are longer, marshal," said the Emperor, with a frown. THE PENALTY OR BEING A FATHER.—When a Chinese lady is blessed with an increase to her family, from the moment of her accouchmeut, the unhappy husband is put to bed also, and there detained for forty days, and during this delightful penance he is subjected to all the rigorous treat- ment of his better half. Should medicine be administered to her, he must partake of it also, and he is strictly confined to the same diet that she is obliged to undergo, which consists on an average, I believe, of about a thimbleful of cream of rice, administered every three hours, to say nothing of the pill at bed-lime to prevent indiges- tion.—Seciie's Reside cc at Siam. Taft DROWSY WATCH.— The Burgomaster of Termonde, in Belgium, having reason to suspect that the police of the town were not so active as they ought to be, got up at an early hour in the morning a few days ago, and found the men, instead of being on duty, snoring in The guard- house. He slipped quietly into the place and possessed himself of each man's sword, and then not clear off. When the policemen awoke, they were astonished to find that their swords were gone, and they looked for them everywhere. They visited all the suspected houses in the town, and made a descent into the lodgings of certain bad characters ; but they could find nothing. The policemen were greatly mortified, and the scoffs and jeers of tha iuhahilants were not wanting. Three days after, the Burgomaster sent for the Commissary of Police, and gave up the swords to him; but he published an order blatning the men for their negligence, and ordering that each of them • should be confined for eight days. MR. TOWNSEND, in his account of the visit of Handel to Dublin, narrates that when at Chester the great musician got together a band, whom he had been assured could "sing at sight," to try his manuscript. On trial of the chorus in the Messiah, "And with his stripes we are healed,' the principal singer, named Janson, after attempting his part several times, failed so egregiously that Handel burst into a rage, and after vociferating in four or five languages, exclaimed in broken English, "You schauntrel I tit you not dell me dat you could sing at soite ? " Yes, sir," replied the unfortunate basso, "and as I can ; but not At first sing."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 25, 193.

Exeter Stew.

Gravy beef or shin, lib. ; onions, two (sliced) ; drip- ping, loz. ; flour, one tablespoonful ; vinegar, one tablespoonful ; water, 1 pints. Make the dripping hot in a imucepan ; add the onions, and fry a nice brown ; sprinkle in the flower and let it brown ; add the vinegar wl ich gives a nice sharp flavour, the meat cut in sliz-es, and the water. Keep on the lid. Let it nearly boil, then simmer till tender—about two hours. If shin of beef be used it will require another hour or longer. A Useful Custom. _

The peasants in iiitt-Lii-WO-uld-se-e-m to bs very frank and honest, for on certain fete days the young ladies appear in red petticoats with white and yellow borders round them. The number of borders denotes the portion the father is willing to give his daughter. Each white band represents silver, and denotes one hundred francs per annum, while each yellow band denotes gold and be- tokens a thousand francs a year. nus, a young man who sees a face that pleases him has but to glance at the trimmings of the petticoats, and without any bashful or suspicious inquiry he can learn what amount of money accompanies the sweet wearer. Bread Pudding. Bread crusts, Alb. ; sugar, 2,z. ; egg, one ; nutmeg, a pinch ; suet, 2z. ; currants 4.. z. ; milk, one teacupful. Soak the bread in cold water, prepare the suet and cur- rants, beat the egg. When the bread is soft, squeeze it quite dry and break up with a fork; add the other ingredients. Pour into a greased pie dish and bake ' about one hour. Turn out when done. This pudding may be boiled ; then it requires little or no milk. Time one-and-half hours. When an egg is not used, two • tablespoonfuls of flour should be added to bind the mixture.

Wedding Dresses on Hire. Time out of mind, as is well known, the hiring out of dress suits has formed the main feature in procuring a fortune for many a wardrobe dealer. It will nevertheless come as a surprise to many to find that garments of all descriptions as " let " for such interesting occasions as weddings, in much the same manner as carriages with a pair of greys. " You'd be astonished," said a man en- gaged in this peculiar calling, " what a number of peo- ple come to me. I let out dresses and suits for all occa- sions, balls, parties, dinners, &c., but my most regular line is what I call the matrimonial department. To many cf my customers about to be married my services are as necessary as the parson's or the registrar's. You mark my words, in a few years the custom of carefully packing away the dress I was married in 'to be shown to the children as they grow up, will be quite a thin; of the past, and the money that would otherwise be spent in baying dresses, probably useless for everyday wear, will be spent in other ways. Why, only last week I had what I call a downright sensible order. There was a dress for the bride, one each for two bridesmaids, and a suit each for the groom and best man, all for one wedd- ing. Why,the turn-out would have cost them about fifteen I pounds if they bought the clothes, whereas they only paid me fifteen shillings. That's the way to save money. Sometimes I get customers who,wanting to cut an extra dash, will hire out a travelling dress, in addition to the wedding dress for the bride. Of course, that's extra. How no I get them hack ? Oh ! the travelling dress is only worn when driving from the house, just to cause the neighbours to Kiake remarks. It is returned with the other the next day. No, I have never lest any; I you see I state on my card, • References necessary.' "

As the reader is aware that squire Markham set out npon his return to i onemoor home two mouths after the date of these letters, and that before leav- ing Jerusalem he had not received them, or any tidings whatever concerning his rand child, we will state here that the packet was safely transported to Jerusalem' but that, as not uufrequentl• happens, it was several weeks upon the way, the mail suff,rine some detention, or the packet being mislaid. It arrived at its destination a few days after his departure, was detained for him nearly a month and then dispatched to its source, arriving at Lonemoor a week after himself. Such being the fate Of the letters, of course the Orkneys and the Quillets naiLed in vain for the ex- pected answers. These weeks of waitine were to Gwen weeks of torture. In spite of the revelation of her origin, her proud spirit remained unbroken. She rode out upon the moors daily, she made no alteration in her mode of life, the Quillets refusing to allow her to do so. But she was galled daily and hourly with innumerable stings. The servants watched her curiously, tuad obeyed her only because if they refused they would be diemissed. The farmers sons leered at her; the farmers' daughters turned away their heads , if she chanced to be in sight. The female Orkneys gossiped about her incessantly,. calling her by their favourite epithets of "beggar and outcast." They excited against her the popular sentiment, so that she was more than once insulted during her rides; and, to crown her humiliation, several of the servants, including her maid, gave up their Situations upon the ground that Lonentoor had ceased to be respectable, now that a person of disreputable origin held sway there. Claxton Orkney, knowing the cloud of obloquy that had gathered around the innocent girt, exhibited a latent spark of manliness. Be quer- relied with his family, and rode to Lonemoor, offering to Gwen, who chanced to be in the garden, his hand in marriage. Gwen refused him. The latent spark died out, and he joined himself to her persecutors, determined to force her into a marriage wi, h him, his love growing upon her op- position. He came to Lonemoor daily. When she saw him coming she retired to her own rooms; but he acquired a habit of coming upon her unawares, sometimes visiting her in the garden in the morning or in the drawing-room in the evening, and not all the vigilance of the (billets could keep him out. Of course the facts of these visits came to the ears of the Orkney family, amid greatly inilatned their hatred of Gwen. But Claxton Or • ney was not Gwen's only visitor. Ronald Chilton came daily to Lonerstoor. He was ignorant of the gossip and calumny circulating in the neighbourhood against Miss Winter. Upon the very day after his rescue of her from the grasp of young Orkney upon the inoor,Mr. Coilton presented himself at Gwen a home, and was met by the butler who inquired his business, announcing himself as Miss Winter's present guardian. Mr. Chilton exhibited certain credentials to Mr. Quillet, and requested permission to see MissWiuter and inquire after her health. Therequest was granted, and Mr. Chilton became a frequent visitor at the house. The Quillets encouraged these visits. "You see, Maria," said the butler one morning, when alone with his wife. "Mr. Chilton is a gentle. man arid highly connected, and he is in love with Miss Gwen. Now if the squire won't own her, or do anything for her, you know, she might marry him and be happy, its an anchor to windward, this love affair, and I hope it'll hold through the storm I foresee." " Ah, yes," sighed the housekeeper, "hut Mr. Chilton doesn't know the girl's story. When he hears it he'll go away, you see." " He's coming now over the moor," exclaimed Mr. Quillet. "I'll meet him in the garden, Maria, and put him to the proof. 11l tell him the story as the Orkneys and others know it, and we'll see what stuff he's made off. The truth is, Maria, I'm getting anxious about our failure to hear from the squire. We ought to have had a letter before this, If Mr. Chilton after having Miss Gwen's history gives up her acquaintance, and if the squire in his anger at us and hatred of her does not answer our letter, then Heaven only knows what is to become of the girl. GENERAL JACKSON WAS DEFEATED 'The only way he cbuld obtain relief was to have a sap ling partially cut and bent down so he could lean over it with his abdomen pressed hard against the tree and as toul of his toes and fingers just touching the ground." In Mercy's name think of anybody being driven to such a resort as that! And yet what does Trouble care for high or low? This was one of the most famous men that ever lived—General Jackson, a military gonui scarcely second to Owns or Napoleon. Yet only te fancy this great general and statesman in the grotesque even ridiculous attitude above described! What could have made this fearless fighter cry for quarter ? 104 /the historian tell. " Genesal Jackson was tortured by acute dyspeopia. and the only way he could obtain relief was to have a sapling partially cut and bent down so be could lean over it, with his abdomen pressed hard against the tree and the ends of his toes and fingers jug' touching the ground." We quote from Mr. Oliver Dyeeg "Life of Jackson," published in 1891. It is clear enough now. In surrendering to such an enemy General Jackson only did what other heroes have done—Napoleon among them. It follows that whoso- ever can cure this disease (which is universal) holds the world in the hollow of his hand. All sorts and condi- tions of men have a stake in this contest; and there hal a woman to whom it isn't as important as the roof over her head.

Here is a straw that shows which way the wind blowS "In August, 1889," writes the witness, "I had pail after eating, and vomited all my food. For a week at a time nothing would stay on my stomach. I had a bad taste in the mouth, and a slimy phlegm would gather ill my mouth, and stick in my throat causing me to be sick. I was tired all day long, and even more so in the morn' lug than when I went to bed at night. After a while I began to be troubled with shortness of breath and 3 sense of fulness or constriction in the throat, so that at times it seemed as if I must choke. There was fearful pain in my left side, and a most oppressive teeling of tightness round my waist and at my chest. I lost a great deal of sleep, and night after night I only dozed for an hour or two. You will easily believe me when I say that I became dreadfully weak and lay in Led pf three weeks at a time. " During my illness I had four doctors attending met yet I grew so bad that one of them called an additional docter in consultation. On this occasion I tnought was going to die, and all who called to see me vere of the same opinion. After I had suffered !a this manner for six maths my brother Alfred told me of the great benefit his wife had received from taking a medicina called Mother Seigel's Syrup, and persuaded me to VI it. I did so, and after having taken a few doses my food digested, and I felt easier and gaiimd some strength' By the time I had used three bottles I was back at nil work, strong and hearty, and have since been in go°cl health. lam a forgeman, and have worked at the Col- brook Forge over sixteen years. I have recommended this remedy to many persons upon whom it b 0 had the same good effect. You have my free consent to palish my letter if you think fit. I will answer inquiries.

LADIES' LETTER

- CAPITAL " CHAT.

Earl Beauchamp, who came of age on Monday, has developed a Krong liking for public work. At Oxford he made his debut by a furious onslaught on Mr. Goschen's Free Education scheme, and has since then added to his reputation as a young man who has both ideas aud language. Some time ago Earl Beauchanip became honorary secretary of the Christ Church Mission at Poplar, and took up his residence in the East end. He is a youth of whom we shall probably hear a gooa deal, for he has the money as well as the gifts and inclination to leava a mark. The estates are mainly in Worcester, and are worth £25,000 a year.

Lady Battersea is not only a charming hostess, but is a woman who recognises that her duties extend beyond her own drawing-room. On Miss Willard's recent visit to Luton Lady Battersea was one of the most fluent and effective speakers, not only urging the cause of temperance, at a public demonstration, but presiding at the meeting, and doing everything she could for its success.

Mr. Jeremiah Jordan, who was returned last Saturday for South Meath, as an anti-Parnellite, is well known in the House of Commons, having sat for West Clare from 1885 till the last General Elec- tion, when he unsuccessfully contested North Fer- managh against Mr. Dane, the Orange member. Mr. Jordan is an ardent Methodist and teetotaler. He is a general merchant in a large and prosperous business in Enniskillen, and he has been chairman of the Enniskillen Town Commissioners. Ile is a thin, wiry, medium-sized man of about sixty. At election meetings in England his plain, unpretenti- ous speeches and moral earnestness produce a great effect.

The desire expressed by Cardinal Vaughan that that no presentation should be made to him by English Catholics on his return home, has, as might have been expected, not been regarded binding by his many friends. Although no money testimonial will be offered to him, opportunity has been found to give expression in another graceful form to the general regard in which the Cardinal is held. Cer- tain prominent members of the Catholic body in London under the lead of Lady Herbert of Lea have undertaken the work of " rehabilitating " Arch- bishop's House.

The private chapel, library, dining-hall, and the Cardinal's private apartments are all being hand- somely refitted and furnished-- a striking contrast to the severe simplicity of Manning's days. Lady Sykes (the wife of Sir Tatton Sykes) takes upon 'herself the appointments ot the grand staircase, and the upholstering and gilding of twenty State chairs n the throne-room; and other distinguished ladies have each selected their own part in the work. Lord Petre presents a handsome carriage and sets of har- ness, and a small committee,comprised of the Duke of Norfolk, the Marquis of Bute, the Lord Mayor of London, Sir Philip Rose,Mr.W.Hearle Lyall, and Mr. Cave, are raising a fund for the purchase of suitable horses, and these gentlemen furthermore guarantee as annual maintenance fund for the same.

• It was a great triumph, no doubt, for the Liberals to win Hexham in such splendid style; but Mr. Clayton, the defeated candidate, is sadly wanted in The House of Commons. After his defeat he addressed the local Tories and said :—" The great wave of Unionism that has swept over the constitu- ency has received a check, but wilt be beard of yet." Sir Boyle Roche himself could hardly have beaten this, and alas ! we have no Sir Boyle Roche nowa- days. Even the "Morning Post,' since the days when it " tore the mask from Cobbett's face and exposed his cloven hoof," has greatly chastened the style of its anti-popular leaders.

• --- M.Berthelot, whose laboratory assistant met with a shocking accident two or three days ago by the bursting of an acid flask, is the most distinguished French chemist living. He must of course not be confounded with Count Berth°llet, the famous dis- coverer of the bleaching properties of chlorine, who in fact, died before the present professor of organic chemistry at the Sorbonne was born. Professor Berthelot is sixty-five years of age, and for many years he has shared with Professor Bunsen, of Heid- elberg, the chief honours of chemical education in Europe, having trained many of our foremost English analysts. A,4 Bunsen is Foreign Associate of the Paris Acaaemy so Berthelot is foreign mem- bar of our own Royal Society. Wh:le he is held in exceptional esteem in the world of sc:qnce for his luminous contributions to chemical tneory, he is best associated in the memory of his countrymen I with the siege of Paris, during which he superin- tended the preparation of explosives of the dyna- mite class for the use of the army of defence. His I experience during these memorable months led to his appointment to a post similar to that held by Sir Frederick Abel, and in this capacity he con- trolled the researches which resulted in the discovery of the smokeless powder now generally used. He is the editor of a learned magazine, a sort of Nature and Philosophical Transactions rolled into one. Mr. W. Frank Calderon, the already well-known son of a celebrated father, who only a year ago married Ethel,daughter of Mr. IL H. Armstead,R.A., is not yet thirty, though well at the top of the artistic tree. He was educated at University College and in 1879 gained the Trevelyan Goodall Scholar- ship,which provided for his art education for three years at the Slade School (under M. Legros.) In 1881 he also gained a Slades Scholarship of i.:50 for three years, and in the same year sent his first pic- ture to the Academy, which was hung on the line, and purchased by the Queen. Since that time he ttns been a constant exhibitor, and was recently elected a member of the Institute of Painters in Oils, and besides his paintings does a great deal of work in black and white, and his also accounted one of the first teachers of animal painting.

Amongst the many objects of interest which would strike Mr. Gladstone's attention in the course of his Windsor visit,on Tuesday evening, he did not find a portrait of himself. Such a thing, in fact, dces not exist in any part ot the Castle, except in the frontispieces to one or two volumes of his in the Royal Library, and in the personal rooms of those members of the rdyal hcusehold who admire him. On the private staircase which intervenes between the Grand Corridor and her Majesty's personal apartments there are six specially-selected paint- ings in oil. Of these four are Court pageants, namely, Mr. Lockhart's Jubilee function at the Abbey,Tuxen's 'Meeting of the Royal Family in the IGreen Drawing Room," Sydney Hall's "Marriage of , the Duke of Connaught,' and Von Werner's " Be- -1 throthal of the Princess Irene." The other two are portraits of the late Earl of Beaconsfield and of the ' vresent Marquess of Salisbury. The Grand Corridor contains one or two living statesmen, and the ab- sence of the present Premier is, therefore, the more I conspicuous.

The young Duke of Aosta's visit to England has no matrimonial object in view. He is a persona grata at Marlborough House, and is much interested in English horses. He is quite a young man, being only twenty-four yeus old. He is tall, wears a dark moustache, is of sallow complexion, and very fine ! physique, which is shown off to best advantage by the picturesque Italian uniform. He received part of his education at Harlow, and when he was quite a baby he spent three years in Spain during the brief reign of his father, the late Duke Amadeo of Aosta, who was made king of Marshal Prim in 1870. The young duke is the stepson of the lovely Letitia Bonaparte, Duchess of Aosta, who created such a sensation in London two or three years ago by her beauty and her diamonds, and who created a still greater sensation in Europe some years before when she married her elderly uncle. The young duke is the nephew of the present king of Italy, and is rather coldly looked at.by the court of the Qnirinal—probably because he is such a much finer youth than the Prince of Naples.

A wedding in which society and all classes of politicians are much interested is that of Sir George Baden Powell with Miss Wilson, the daughter of Mr. Charles Wilson, of Cheltenham, who must not be confused with Mr. Charles Wilson, M.P., ot Grosvenor-sq.,the brother of Mr. Arthur Wilson, of Tranby Croft. Sir George is one of the most ener- getic and popular politicians of the day, and in society he is known as a fluent and interesting talker. He was educated at Marlborough and Balliol, won his spurs as Assistant-Governor of Vic- toria, and was last to the fore in the Behring Sea bueiness. He is a fair, clean-shaven, dapper little man, who looks much younger than his real age, which is forty-six. He has been M.P., for Kirkdale (Liverpool) since 1885. His fiancee is a very charm- 'lug girt who has been a belle in Cheltenham, and will be an acquisition to the list of London hostesses.

DICAREST

NELLIE.

The new arrangement of hair makes the fixing on of tiaras a matter of some difficulty, but not insurmountable. Diamond stars have the merit of being easily settled in any kind of coiffure ; but, though pretty and becoming, toey are by no means so imposing as tiaras. Some girls much affect a flat bow of velvet, corresponding in colour either with their gown or its trinunings. This is pia on wire covered with velvet, and curved to fit round the coils of hair at the back. Sometimes this velvet is very finely wrought in gold, silver, jet, or jewPllery, and also it often serves as a background tor acme sparkling diamond ornaments. Girls quite young wear jewels now, although in modest quantity and simple form. Tiaras, sprays, stars, ilielems, and single•stone necklets are donned only with the dignity of two score years, but with' matronhood, however early in life that may be attained.

The newest millinery is very gay. The straw hats are mostly in two shades, btu& and vieux roses, green and white, &c., and the light tone is generally carried out again in the trimmings. A smart chapeau of black straw with crown of green, and a band to match on the brim, was gay with green velvet twists and bows, and black ostrich tips, whilst a black and heliotrope shape was adorned with shaped mauve and purple poppies. The general effect of the vivid colours obtaining is cheery and bright, and individually the brilliant coloura are most becoming. One lady at a recent reception was garbed in bright scarlet, which with magnificent diamonds made quite a regal display. An entire dress of turquoise blue fancy silk and velvet, with which diamonds and turquoises were worn, was much admired. A gown of bright purple velvet looked beautiful, and was by no means the single instance in which that hue was adopted either for an entire dress or for trimming gowns of other colour. A beautiful frock was in bright strong yellow brooatelle, the embossed design being very rich and effective. The skirt was wide and full, and was just on the ground at the back. It was trimmed with five narrow ruches of the palest possible green velvet placed at graduated interval e round the skirt to within about three-quarters of a yard of the waist. Toe bodice was crossed over and made with picture sleeves. Over these were deep frills of pale green chiffon embroidered round the edge with silk. The effect was very fresh and spring-like, and the gown an exec4ively pretty one.

A toilette de ville, in dark green tartan velvet, with Princess overdress in drab cloth laced down the right side was charming to contemplate, despite the absence of glaring colours. A kind of deep double collar extended in cascade folds to just below the waist, and a tiny embroidery ornamented the edges of this and the tunic part. Evening gowns are now often made entirely of velveteen in some of the charming new tints. Moss and lizard shades are exzeedingly fashionable, and sapphire-blue is always a favourite with blondes. One gown was of moss-green velveteen, with a cascade ruche, pouffed short sleeves, and a folded belt all of the same material. The front of the bodice was turned back to form revels, and folds of silk were inserted. This gown may be renderod exceedingly smart if the shell or cascade ruche were lined with pink surah or merv, and the skirt made up on pink linen or alpaca, and finished with a pink bayaleuse. The opening of the bodice could also be tilled in with pink, and the hair dressed with little ban( -4 pink heather.

For home and unceremonious dinners it is quite permissible to wear a teagown. Often these gar- ments, which were originally intended for deshabille, are marvels of rich materials and artistic creation, d fficu I t to distinguish from veritable dinner costumes. The Empire style is the favourite, although the Watteau runs it very close, and is certainly the more becoming of the two for figures which are not quite perfect. One was of silver grey satin brocadesi in silver, opening over a petticoat of very pale rose-coloured satin, clouded in white ; round the hem a band of white fox for; on the shoulders a deep fall of iseedle-run Alen9on lace, with a narrow band of fur at the top; sleeves with large pulls to the elbow, and from thence tight to the waist. Another gown was of heliotrope silk.corduroy—a new and very pretty material, made very pretty with a Watteau pleat at the back and a lace yoke in the front, trimmed round the hem with three closely-pleated frills of white crepe, the sleeves and neck to match. A third costume was of a rich, handsome. looking fabric, silk poplin, m the shade of green known as Vert Isly. The lace of the under-dress, wlich showed as chemisette, and ion, tight-fitting cuffs on bodice, and again, through the gores of skirt, was deep cream, almost coffee-colour, and the velvet chestnut brown. Yours ever, JENNig.

The plans of Lord Salisbury's villa in the South of France were prepared by Lady Salisbury. Laces not in wear should be dabbled in clear cold water to remove all traces of starch, dried in the sun, and wrapped in dark-blue paper.

A law in Turkey, with penalties in case of infringe- ment, declares it to be a great offence for Mohammedan women to be photographed.

There is a large carriage manufactory in Di aw York in which tile chief decorator is a woman, who has twenty women employed in the department which she controls.

WARRINGTON LITERARY AND PIIILOSOPRICAL SOCIETY.

PAPER ON RENWICK. IBSEN.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Literary and Philosophical Society was held on Monday in the Museum Lecture Room. The chair was occupied by the president of the society (Mr. Robert Davies,) and the essayist was Mr. T. C. London, who gave an essay on the Norwegian dramatist, Renrick Ibsen. Mr. J. H. Jackson, and Mr. J. G. Garnall were proposed as members of the society. Mr. Davies in his opening remarks said that he was almost perfectly ignorant of the subject to be dis- cussed that night, and he came there expecting to learn something. Mr. LONDON then proceeded with his essay. Ho said that an Englishman's culture rarely extended to a know- ledge of continental literature. It sometimes extended to a somewhat slight knowledge of the French literature. We need a little widening of our horizon, and in coming that night to study the subject of Ibsen they came to study a man destined to exercise a great influence Upon the thought of the world. We cannot understand Ibsen's works, unless we know something about the man. Ibsen was born in a village in Norway, called Skeen, on March 20, 1828. It may be said that he had not a drop of Norwegian blood in his veins by direct descent. From what we know of his descent, there was a mix- ture of Danish, German, and Scotch in his constitution. This descent throws some considerable light upon his character. From the Scotch element, we have that remarkable puritan vein running through all his works. From the German are derived the speculativeness mani- fested in nearly all his works, and the tendency to deal with abstract and abstruse questions of morality with regard to the higher branches of human conduct. Skeen was only a homely little town of 3,000 inhabitants, but there was an upper class, consisting of the officials of the municipality, and the older families, and there was also a lower class. Ibsen's family belonged to the former, but when he was about eight years if age, the fortunes of the family were broken, and his father became bankrupt. Ibsen's youth was remarkable for gravity and lack of familiarity with associations of his boyhood. He had a very distinct artistic. faculty,and had a longing to be an artist. He was sent to a public school, and even here he distinguished himself. He afterwards went to Grimsted as an apothecary. His earliest published poem was written here in 1849, upon the Hungarian de- feat, in which he glorified those who were slain. His first plays were received very coldly by the theatre managers and the public, but he finally won his way into public esteem. Mr. London commented very severely upon the criticism which Ibsen met with in England, and concluded his paper by giving a sketch of one of Ibsen's works entitled 'Brand." In the discussion vrnich followed, Mr. DAVIES said that he had the greatest admiration for the ancient . Greeks, whe gave us the noblest features not only of the human mind as exemplified by her philosophers, but most beautiful features of the human form, but he he did not like such studies as he gathered from the essayist, that Ibsen indulged in. He wanted to look upon something bright and noble, and did not want to hear anything repulsive. There must have been some- thing that repelled certain people from Ibsen. and he (Mr. Davies), thought that he himself would be one of those people. At the same time, he thought the essayist had shown great ability in his paper that night.—Mr. A. J. JOLLEY moved a vote of thanks to the essayist. He said that he understood Mr. London to compare Ibsen with Zola. If that comparison be a just one, Ibsen was not deserving of the high praise which Mr. London had showered upon him. It was very fine for writers to stir up the mud, and then to put in a few lines of moral sentiment at the finish.—Mr. A. BENNETT seconded, and Mr. J. PEERS, and Mr. J: RYMER YOUNG supported the motion, which was carried unanimously.—Mr. LONDON briefly replied, and Mr. DAVIES, referring to Mr. J. G. Go( nail, one of the new members proposed for election to the society, complimented him on the way in which he had grappled with the smallpox epidemic in War- rington. and expressed satisfaction that the disease had been almost stamped out. The meeting then termi- nated.

STUMBLING ON FORTUNES

Gold was discovered in California in 1848, and in Colorado in 1858. The discovery was accidental in both cases, and the fact created the impression that mines were "lying about loose." Adventurers drifted about in hopes of stumbling upon a mine. Here are some instances of lucky finds ;—Three men while looking for gold in Calitornia discovered the dead body of a man who evidently had neen" prospecting,." "Poor fellow," said one of the trio, " he has passed in his checks." "Let's give him a decent burial," said another. "Some wife or mother will be glad if ever she imows it." They began todig a grave. Three feet below the surface they discovered signs of gold. The stranger was buried in another place and where they had located a grave they opened a gold mine.—An adventurer who had drifted 0 Leadville awoke one morning without food or money. He went out and shot a deer, which, in its dying agonies kicked up the dirt and disclosed signs of gold. The poor man staked out a "claim," and opened one of the most profitable claims ever worked in Lead ville.—Dead Man Claim," the name given to another rich mine in Leadville, was discovered by a broken-down miner while digging a grave. —A miner died when there was several feet of snow on the ground. His comrades laid his body in a snow bank and hired a man for 4dols. to dig a grave. The gravedigger, after three days' absence, was found digging a mine instead of a grave ; while excavating he had struck gold.—An unsuccessful Australian miner went up and down Colorado for several months " pro- specting" for gold and finding none. One day he sat down open a stone, and, while musing over his hard luck, aimlessly struck a stmie with his pick. !Ie chipped off a piece and sprang to his feet—the chip was rich gold quartz. He hurried into the little town of Rosita and went to the assay office, where a teamster had just dumped a load of wood. fie agreed to saw the wood to pay for assaying his chippea sample. The result of the assay sent him back to his "claim." When he had taken out of it 500,000d015. he sold the mine for 400,000d015. in cash and 1,000,000d015. in stock.

The Treasury shall publish not less than six months previous notice of the transfer of Savings Banks, so that depositors and trustees may, if they so elect, be dealt with by Great Britain. DECISION OF CONSTITUTIONAL QUESTIONS. Clause 22 deals with this subject : The appeal from courts in Ireland to the House of Lords shall cease ; and where any person would, but for this Act, leave a right to appeal from any court in Ireland to the House of Lords, such person shall have the like right to appeal to her Majesty the Queen in Council. The 23rd clause contains a special provision. If it appears to the Lord-Lieutenant or a Secretary of State I expedient in the public interest that steps shall be taken for the speedy determination of the question whethe any Irish Act or any provision thereof is beyond the powers of the Irish Legislature he may represent the same to her Majesty in Council, and thereupon the said question shall be forthwith referred to and heard and determined by the Judical Committee of the Privy Council, constituted as if hearing an appeal from a court in Ireland.

===== 0002601_18930225_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
TIER JUDGES.

.--„--. The jadges appointed after this Act are to be irremov- able, except in pursuance of an address from the two Houses. The present judges are to continue in office, but if any of them retires with the Queen's approbation before completion of the period of service entitling him to a pension, her Majesty may, if she thinks fit, grant to him such pension.

CIVIL SERVICE. All existing officers in the permanent civil service of the Crown shall continue to hold their offices by the same tenure and to receive the same salaries, gratuities, and pensions, and to be liable to perform the duties as heretofore, or such duties as the Treasury may declare to be analagous ; and the said gratuities and pensioas, and until three years after the passing of this Act, the salaries due to any of the said officers, if remaining in his existing office, shall be paid to the payees. Any such officer may after three years from the pass- ing of this Act retire from office, and shall at any time during those three years, if required by the Irish Government, retire from office, and on any such retire- ment may be awarded by the Treasury a gratuity or pension.

*IRISH LAND. The thirty-fifth clause introduces a temporary restric- tion regarding the land question. During three years from the passing of this Act, and if Parliament is then sitting until the end of that session of Parliament, the Irish L.,gislature shall not pass an Act respecting the relations of landlord and tenant, or the sale, purchase letting of land generally ; provided that nothing in this section shall preaent the passing of any Irish Act with a view to the purchase of land for railways, harbours, waterworks, town improvements, Cr other local under- takings. TEMPORARY APPOINTMENT OF IRISH JUDGES. During six years from the passing of this Act the appointment of a judge of the Supreme Court or other superior Court in Ireland (other than one of the Exchequer judges) shall be made in pursuance of a warrant from her Majesty, countersigned as here- to-fore.

THE MEETING OF THB IRISH PARLIAMENT. The Irish Legislature shall be summoned to meet on the first Tuesday in September, 1894, and the first elec- tion of members of the two Houses of the Irish Legisla- ture shall be held at such a time before that day as may be fixed by her Majesty in council. There are forty clauses and seven schedules in the Bill.

AN APPEAL TO THE IRISH RACE. The " Weekly Sun " ccntains the text of an appeal to America and Australia for the collection of funds for the remainder of the Home Rule campaign. The appeal, which has been cabled to Dr. Thomas Addis Emmet and other officers of the Irish Federation of the United States and to organisations in Canada and Australia for distri- bution among the supporters of Home Rule in those countries, is signed by Mr. Justin M'Carthy (chairman). Edward Blake, Thomas Sexton, Michael Davitt, John Dillon, T. M. Healy, William O'Brien, Arthur O'Connor, and T. P. O'Connor, as the Committee of the Irish Parliamentary party " We have reached," the address says, "a most crucial moment in the history of Ireland's long struggle for her rights." It describes the Home Rule Bill as forming as a whole a broad, solid, and enduring plan of national self-government. placing Ireland on a higher national plane than the bill of 1886. The representatives of Ireland, c‘intinues the appeal, have accepted without hesitation the constitution pro- posed in this bill as a fitting consummation of the sacri- fices and labours of the Irish race for so many centuries ; and they believe they could regard the enactment of this measure as a " final and triumphant close of the long, bloody, and sorrowful struggle." They cannot, how- ever, disregard the possibility of a prolonged and desperate campaign to defeat Mr. Gladstone's noble efforts, and wreck the Irish cause. Confronted by enemies venomous, unscrupulous, and with boundless wealth, it is impossible for the party to carry on even the short remnant of the struggle without assistance from all parts of the world. The appeal concludes by saving that, " borne by the generosity of their race through the long night, they ask now for the aid required for the hi ief interval that still stands between Ireland and her breaking day."

ULSTER AND THE BILL.

The Lord Mayor of Belfast on Wednesday afternoon issued the following :—" In pursuance of a requisition, very numerously and influentially signed, received by me, of which the following is a copy :—• To Sir Daniel Dixon, J.P., Lord Mayor of Belfast,—We, the under- signed, request that your lordship would call a public meeting of merchants, tradesmen, and other ratepayers in Belfast who are opposed to the establishment of a separate legislature in Ireland, in order that they may have an opportunity of expressing their views on this very important subject,l request a public meeting of the merchants, traders, and other ratepayers of thecity who are opposed to the establishment of a separate legisla- tive body in Ireland, to be held in the covered market, St. Mary's-st., on Tuesday evening, the 28th, at half. past seven o'clock.—DANlEL DIXON, Lord Mayer."

It is proposed in Belfast that a deputation of 5,000 Ulstermen should proceed to London at their own expense and demand from Mr. Gladstone that he and his party shall not separate Ulster from the rest of the empire by his Home Rule Bill without their consent. No threats or violent language are to be used, but the demonstration is to act as a warning to the Premier.

--- A leading Conservative member of the House of Com- mons on Wednesday received a letter from an influen- tial Irish frieni stating that in every church in the diocese of Down, Conner, and Dromore, prayers were off i offered last Sunday against Home Rule. It s added that arrangements are being made to hole meetings and pass resolutions in every town and village throughout Ulster against Home Rule, these to be followed by a shoal of petitions to Parliament.

CANADIAN IRISHMEN AND Horn RULE. Orrawa, WEDNESDAY.—A. meeting of the Irish resi- dents of this city was held last night, when addresses were given by the Hon. J. W. Curran, Solicitor-General, and the Hon. John Costigan, Secretary of State, and several members of the Dominion Parliament. It was decided that steps should be taken to raise mosey in aid of the Irish party supporting the Home Rule Bill. Toirm.rro, WEDNESDAY.—At a meeting of the Canadian branch of the Irish National League, held last night, a resolution was unanimously adopted approving the principles cf Mr. Gladstone's Home Rule Bill, subject to the modifications desired by the Irish Parliamentary party.

MR. DILLON'S OPINION.

Speaking on Wednesday at a meeting of the Irish National Federation in Dublin, Mr. Dillon, M.P., re- ferring CO the Rome Rule Bill, said they had in it a measure which would give Ireland a freer Constitution and a more national government than she had under 'Grattan's Parliament. The Bill was one which justified any experienced politician in hoping for an arrange- ment under it by which the two countries would be able to develop national life in full partnership in the days to come. He did not say it was a perfect measure, but it offered to Ireland greater liberty than had ever been offered to her before. MIDLOTHIAN AND THE BILL. The Executive Committee of the Midlothian Liberal Association, meeting in Edinburg on Wednesday, were asked by Mr. John Romans, convener for Newbattle dis- trict, to pass a resolution to the effect "that the part of the Irish Home Rule Bill which legalises the retention ot eighty Irish representatives in the Imperial Parliament, where their votes can be used to upset and undo measures of vast importance to Scotland, whilst the Scottish members have no such powers over Irish measures, would be an act of injustice to Scotland, and would assuredly lead to national dissatisfaction, which would destroy confidence between the two peoples; and that no Ho,,e Rule measure is equitable and practicable which does not confer upon Scotland as well as upon Ireland a separate Legislature and Executive for the management of their respective national affairs, and give representatives in the Imperial Parliament along with England for managinc, Imperial business." The resolu- tion was only supported by the mover and his son. The meeting adopted another resolution congratulating Mr. Gladstone on his measure of Home Rule, and hailing with pleasure the prospect of reform in registration and

other important matters.

THE SHEFFIELD MURDER. SHOCKING SPECTACLE.

On Saturday Edward Hemmings, collier, was charged at Sheffield with the murder of his wife at Woodhouse. —Superintendent Beilby said the prisoner and his wife for the past five weeks lodged at the house of a man named Charles Edward Kennington, a shunter, occupy- ing a room down stairs as a sleeping and living room. They retired to bed at ten o'clock on the night of the 15 Ii apparently friends. About three o'clock the following, morning a man named George Bradshaw, who lodged in the house, heard a slight scream proceeding from the room occupied by the prisoner and his wife. Knowing that the wife was subject to fits, he was not alarmed, but thinking that she was ill and that assistance might be required, he went to the top of the stairs and shouted, "Ted, is all right ? " The prisoner replied, "Yes ; nowt, nowt." Nothing more was heard until Mrs. Ken- nington heard the slamming of the back door at four o'clock. Knowing that was the time prisoner usually went to work in the pit, he having some distance to go, no notice was taken. About half-past five Bradshaw came downstairs and knocked at the door in passing to go to work to ask the wife if she was all right. Getting no reply he went inside, and was horrified to find the lifeless body of the prisoner's wife laid on her side with her head towards the fireplace, covered by the bed- clothes, the clothes having been removed from the bed. Having removed the clothes he found the woman was dead, her head having been nearly severed from her body. On the floor was a pool of blood. A communica- tion was made to the police, and inquiries were insti- tuted. On Friday night about ten o'clock prisoner went to the police-station at Normanton, where he has friends residing in the district. There he made a communica- tion to the inspector's wife. Inspector Turton, being convinced that the prisoner was the man wanted for the murder, arrested him. Witness had charged him with having murdered his wife and he replied "It's right." The superintendent added that since then the prisoner was examined, and a pair of stockings he was wearing were found to bear marks of blood. He then asked that the prisoner might be remanded until next Friday to enable him to have the inquest held, and also to pi epare the evicbnce necessary to lay before the justices.—Pri- soner was then remanded.

RUGBY.

WARRINGTON v. KENDAL HORNETS.

A visit to the home of Rugby football in Westmoreland is always enjoyed by the Warringtonians, and despite the fact that rain fell the whole time they were at Kendal on Saturday, the outing was thoroughly enjoyed. The match was the return engagement, and there would be a little over 1,000 present when the teams turned up, Heesom and Nevins being still away from the Warring- ton side, but the homesters received the valuable assist- ance of T. Hine, the county full-back, who had resolved to retire from the sport. The heavy rains which had Pr

prevailed in the district rendered the ground heavy going, and of course allowances had to be made for the mistakes which occasionally cropped up.

Allen kicked off and Webster's reply was met by Wilson, the result of the exchanges being that Kendal invaded the Warrington quarters. From the first scrimmage formed the visitors packed the better, and wheeling their opponents round, Holmes, Fair. Turner, and Donohue led the way to the Hornets' quarter, the former flooring Wilson after a quick follow-up. James Bate upset a movement between Hill and Railton, and then a free kick fell to Kendal, wnich Donohue neutralized, and Warrington now set up a permanent attack. Hine partially cleared on several occasions. but Evans, Lawless, and Webster responded brilliantly, whilst Boscow at his first essay intercepted a dribbling venture of Parsons and Hine, a judicious punt giving his side an immense advantage. Evans ran to within ten yards of the line, when the ground accounted for him, and Railton and Allen got the oval away, Crossley helping the sequence with a useful punt. Boscow, however, had to be accounted for, and the Warringtonian assisted his forwards splendidly with his fine kicking, rarely failing to find touch, The Brothers Turner and Jolley led the way in one rush, and then James Bate was penalized, but the advantage did not assist Kendal, for Boscow had to deal with the return, and Warrington were again on the aggressive. Parsons dribbled out of danger, and again Boscow made the " Insects " beat a hasty retreat, whilst from a line- out Jolley got into his stride, and sound tackling upset his well-meant pass. Warrington were again penalized near the line, and Boscow was in a rare position for a drop at goal, but he preferred touch, and his kick gravitated into touch a yard from the corner flag. A Splendid display of passing by the Bates, Evans, and Webster saw the latter being well held before he could get going, and then Higham, Fair. Turner, and Berry were each within an ace of scoring. Jolley was the first in another raid on the Kendal goal, but Hine was safe, and the two Bates passed across the scrimmage, but Crossley intervened, and his kick was responded to by Webster, the first minor being sent up. Evans again ran to the line, whilst James Bate was knocked into touch on the verge of scoring, and from a line-out immediately after Thornilev got over, but the point was overruled. The Kendal backs now passed admirably, and the effort resulted in them reaching the Warriugton quarters for the second time. The return to the attack was quickly affected, and from a scrimmage John Bate got in, but again a deaf ear was turned to the appeal. However, Success attended Warrington on the stroke of the interval, for passing by the backs finally saw Evans transferring to Lawless, and this player got in near the corner, Webster failing at a diffi- cult place, the score at half-time reading—Warrington, 0-1-2; Kendal Hornets, nil. On resuming, the homes- ters adopted the rushing tactics, and found them to act well, and for a time Boscow had all his work cut out to save his lines. The attempts to invade were met with commendable cvolness by the W arrington custodian, and his play was looked upon as one of the most remarkable expositions given at this enclosure. However, as a result of hard work, Kendal reached the Warring- ton quarters, and Thornilev cleared splendidly, starting a dribbling essay, which termi.lated in the visitors at- tacking strongly. Webster responded to a kick by Hine ! and had Warrington only picked up here, a scare was a certainty. The Turners and Ashton were prominent parties in seine heavy forward work, but Allan, Railton, and Wilson passed well, the latter getting clear, but Boscow tackled him finely opposite the grand stand. ! Boscow was now held near the posts, and by degrees the visitors rushed their opponents until they had once more obtained a footing in the Kendal quarters. Thornley again demonstrated himself in a line-out, and Webster was direetly afterwards applauded for a capital save, the attack of Kendal resulting in a minor. From now the visitors forced themselves on the Kendal lines, and the play fluctuated between the quarter flag and the line, and the result of their efforts was but another minor, and an interesting game ended in a Victory for Warrington by 0-1-3 to 0-0-1. Teams :—Warrington : J. Boscow, back ;P. R. Lawless, J. W. Evans, and H. Webster, three-quarter ; John Bate and James Bate, half backs ; H. Ashton, Fair Turner, Frank Turner, J. Jolley, T. Donohue, Tharniley, J. Berry, Holmes, and J. Higham, forwards. Kendal Hornets: T. Hine, back ; Allan, Railton, Wilson, and Crossley, three-quarters ; Hill and Braithwaite, half backs ; Hinson, Nicholson, Whiteley, Simm, W. Wilson, Parsons, Fisher, and F. Hind.

Ruaiconal V. BRADFORD.—On Saturday what is termed the event of the season in local football circles took place in the return engagement between Runcorn and Bradford, on the Irwell-lane enclosure. The first fixture at Park Avenue, on September 10th, was won by the Yorkshiremen, who scored two tries to one, after a most stubborn game. For the match on Saturday, Bradford played the same team, with the exception of Jenkinson and Bailey, that had vanquished Batley on the previous Saturday. Runcorn was also strongly represented. Notwithstanding the inclemency of the weather, which fortunately improved before the kick-off, there would be between four and five thousand spectators present when the teams faced each other as follows :—Bradford • H. Ward, back; Dobson, Johnson, and Rlgg, three- quarter backs; W. Hall, and T. Whitham, half backs; 'Foothill, Broadley, Fieldhouse, Firth, Bates, Hutton, Robertson, Harker, and Kelsey, forwards. Runcorn : Houghton, back ; Rev. G. H. Kingston, J. H. Crompton, and E. J. Morris, three-quarter backs; S. Abrams and Faulkner, half backs . • W. Faulkner, Riley, Farmer, Langley, Williams, Hampson, Taylor, Stubbs, and Davies, forwards. Bradford won the toss, and elected to defend the town goal,which gave them the advantage of the strong wind. W. Faulkner s.arted, Ward returning to Morris, who ran the ball to the Bradford quarters. Toothill got off from a line out, and dribbled to Cromp- ton, who stayed his progress. The visitors still asserted themselves forward, and by short rushes forced the home team to defend. For a considerable length of time heavy scrimmaging ensued, the ball being held very tight, which made the game rather dull. Abrams at length raised the siege, and passing to Morris, that player made a brilliant effort, and ran near the centre line, where he passed to Crompton, who took up the running, and outpacing the whole of the Bradford backs, scored one of the grandest tries that has ever been re- corded on the Runcorn ground, a feat which was warmly applauded. The place kick failed. Resuming, Bradford made another raid on the home line, but some effective passing between the home backs gave Morris a chance, who cleared all opposition, but was finely overhauled close to the line. Toothill, Sugden, and Robertson cleared the danger with a fine dribble, Morris saving by kicking into touch. Crompton next got off with a grand dodgy run, but the tackling was safe. Another bout of passing between the home backs gave Kingston a grand chance, but he was over-raced and hauled down when almost over. Now the Tykes made 'a big rush, and Robertson headed a grand dribble, getting in owing to a misunderstanding between the home backs. Firth was , entrusted with the kick, and landed a goal. From the I centre kick the Runcornians made a supreme effort, and pressing Ward, the latter with a feeble kick put the ball in J. Faulkner's hands, who scored an easy try. Cromp- ton took the place, but again the kick failed. Half-time arrived with the score Runcorn two tries (four points) ; Bradford one goal (five points). Upon resuming, Run- corn played most brilliantly, and the ball was carried to the Bradford lines, Crompton dropping a goal, but on appeal the referee aecided that the ball had passed out- side the post Crompton again got hold, and dodging in ' his own inimitable style, he raced brilliantly .over. But again the kick failed. Runcorn were now leading, but did not in the least slacken their efforts, still keeping up a permanent attack on the Bradford citadel. Passing between Whitham, Hall and Dobsoa gave a little relief, but it was only momentory, Riley, Langley, Farmer, and Taylor led another attack, which resulted in J. Faulkner getting in behind the posts, after some scrimmaging on the Bradford line. Crompton this time placed with suc- cess. Hardly a minate elapsed ere Kingston, charging Dobson's kick down, picked up, and dodging Ward, com- pleted a beautiful try. The kick failed. The Yorkshire- men, stung by this reverse, made a desperate effort to stem the tide of disaster, and a strong rush carried the warfare to the home quarters. Hall got nicely away, and passed to Broadley, who had been put out as extra threequarters, but the Runcorn custodian fairly" spread- eagled" his man. Time was now called, leaving Run- corn the winners of the fastest and most brilliant game that has been played at Runcorn for a considerable length of time. The score read as follows :—Runcorn, 1-4-2 (13 paints) ; Bradford, 1-0-2 (5 points). WIDNES v. BLACELEY.—The above teams faced each other on Saturday afternoon on the Lowerhouse-lane ground. Play was not commenced until at least twenty minutes after advertised time. The visitors kicked off against the wind, and at once pressed the home boys, who, however, put on their spurs, and gained ground, Barber leading the way with a determined rush. Scrim- maging ensued, and Drummond made a brave attempt at goal. The Widnesians continued to press on, the enemy making every effort to withstand the attack,with the result that play was centred about midfield for some time, but eventually they were forced back again, the chemical boys having matters very much their own way. Wright put in a splendid run, but was floored on the very threshold of home, which was very hard lines, but the game was now one-sided,Drummond succeeding in scoring twice, Barber improving on one occasion. During the second half Wright scored twice, and Clarke and Hughes once each, but the attempts at goal were futile. Final result : Widnes, one goal ,five tries ; Black- ley, nothing.

FRIENDS' SCHOOL, PENKSTH V. FARNWOATH GRAILMAR SCHOOL.—This match was played at Penketh on Satur- day. The ground was a veritable puddle, and the game was a rather slow one. Penketh lost the toss, and kicked off, with the wind at their backs, and son scored. At half time the score was four to none in their favour. On crossing over, and playing against the wind, they scored faster, Smith five, Reynolds and T heobald three each, and the game ended in a win for Penketh, 12 to none.

OPENING OF A NEW TEMPERANCE HALL.

On Tuesday afternoon, Mrs. F.W.Willis presided at the opening service of a Temperance Hall, in con- nection with the above-named mission. There was a good attendance of ladies present. The working- men who have been carrying on the outdoor work of this mission are endeavouring to rescue those who were the victims of intoxicating drink and have found a great hindrance to their work during inclement weather in not having a suitable build- ing in which to hold their meetings. The present building is the top room ot the late Wesleyan Sun- day school, which has been renovated at a cost of 'of about .ElOO, and which now seems eminently suitable for the purpose intended. Mrs. Willis in a few remarks said she thought they all saw the need for temperance work in this town, especially, seeing that Warrington was reputed as be- ing the fifth most drunken town in England. She hoped the Government would take the temperance question in , hand, and have public houses governed in a better way than at present. The Bishop of Manchester's scheme seemed the most feasible. The Bishop of Chester's Gothenberg system although an excellent one was rather more difficult to work. She said she had heard that this work in Warrington was taken up by working- men four years ago. They had stood at street corners and besought men to give up the drink. It was thought desirable to have a room in which to hold their meetings and she thought it their duty to show sympathy with this great work. Letters of arology for non-attendance were read from Miss Pier lit y i, Miss Harvey, Mrs. Garnett (Penketh and Mrs. eton. Mrs. Lane rendered two solos dur- ing the a oon. Miss BESSIE DUTTON said she thought the working- men had done the wrong thing in trusting their first meeting to women. (Laughter.) They were gathered together to remind each other of what they could accom- plish. They must not shrink back, or think they had no genius or capacities,for they had both. In:giving a short history of the work of the mission, Miss Dutton said the men had seen around them what temptations were placed right in the way. They also saw there were a hundred inducements on the other side ready to entice them away. But they felt there were many people,who.if they were urged and helped, would give drink up. This was the state of things when the promoters of the Mission began and they thought the very best thing they could do was to stand in the streets and lanes or any places where they could reach the people. They had also had the aid of men from other towns. The men had contributed liberally themselves, and sometimes the Temperance Federation had come to their assistance. Bat they had ielt the want of a room where they could carry on their work during inclement weather, or after the meeting when the pledge was taken and counsel was needed. They had faith in their fellow-townspeople that they would help them, and let none of them think that they could not do anything. Miss Dutton stated that over £93 had been spent to renovate the hall ; a little over LSO had been subscribed. She concluded by making an earnest appeal for funds. Mr. Taylor, Police Court missionary, gave an earnest temperance address. In this movement, he said, there was needed sympathy, unity and perseverance. Votes of thanks to Mrs. Willis, Miss Dutton, and Mrs. Lane concluded the meeting. THE EVENING MEETING.

In the evening a crowded meeting was held in the same hall when Mr. R. Garnett, J.r., occupied the chair. There were also on the platform the Revs. E. T. Reed and J. J. Langham,Mrs. Lewis of (Blackburn), Councillor Dixon, Messrs. John Fairclough, William Roberts, H. Taylor (Police Court Missionary), R. Storey, T. Price, J. Drury, and others. The Revs. John Vonge and T. Ross, sent letters of apology for non- attendance. The CHAIRMAN said he caine for the purpose of show- ing sympathy towards the Temperance Society, but he regretted the circumstances which caused him to occupy the position of Miss Bessie Dutton, who was taken un- well that afternoon and unable to preside at that meet- ing. She was a lady whom he held in great esteem for her works' sake. Speaking with regard to drink, he said they all considered this evil an important one. Even those who were most interested in the sale of drink were ' ready enough to say that they disliked drunkards ex- ceedingly. In regard to the remedy they differed—some ' went in for legal enactment, other friends went in for moral suasion. In his opinion they should go in for moral suasion. There were others who differed on the point—that a little was a good thing. But he believed there was no good in it. It was therefore their duty to do what they could to remove the evil. (Applause.) Mrs. Lewis in an interesting spent' gave many de- tails of her temperance work in Blackburn. She also referred to the use of wine at Sacraments in many churches, but she could not see hi it a symbol of the blood of Jesus Christ, when it was so intimately con- nected with the misery and drunkenness in our midst. Referring to Bands of Hope, she was of opinion that they should not be merely got up for entertainments, but that temperance principles should be well grounded in the children who attended them. The Rev. J. ,T. LANOMAM said he was glad to see the workingmen in homes of their own. He thought it was a grand thing when a man resolved to be a man and not the slave of his body, but it was a still grander thing when he was not content with being that, but resolved to help those brethren who were striving to fight against sin. He believed the workingmen were carrying on this work in the spirit of self sacrifice. Ile hoped they would not be discouraged in the work. The Rev. E. T. REED, who occupied the chair during the latter part of the meeting, said he considered the recommending of strong drink as a medicine by doctors to their patients was a step in the wrong direction. Science was proving morn and more clearly that it was unnecessary as a medicine. In proof of this he stated that there was a hospital which, for the last 10 or 12 years, had been worked on temperance lines, and it was found that patients recovered quite as quickly and well as in any other hospital. The speaker referred to the inspiration he had received on temperance by reading the biography of the late Mrs. Booth: He considered the Band of Hope work the most promising and it ought to be worked on the lines of its founder, Mr. Joseph Livesey, of Preston. Miss ROFF rendered two solos in an admirable manner during the evening. Votes of thanks were accorded the Chairmen, and all who had taken part in the meeting on the proposition of Mr. Drury, seconded by Mr. Price.

_ WELSH DISESTABLISHMEN T.

A crowded meeting on the subject of the disestablish- meet and disendowment of the Church in Wales was heid on Wednesday night at Shrewsbury. The principal speaker was Mr. Lloyd George, M.P., who seconded a resolution expressing approval of the introduction of the Suspensory Bill, as a preliminary to disestablishment and disenaowment, and urging the Government to carry it into law during the present session. Mr. George's speech was in the main a reply to the recent utterances of the Bishop of St. Asaph. With reference to the posi- tion in Parliament, he said he did not believe that a Liberal Ministry could long exist which did not honestly attempt to liberate Wales from the spiritual thraldom she so abhorred. Using no words of menace, and rely- ing upon the honour and good faith of Ministers, he ventured to repeat what he said at Liverpool, that Wales was prepared if need be to cut adrift from her most cherished political connections in order to concentrate her energies on the attainment of that object. The fer- ment aroused during the past few weeks by the mere rumour of hesitancy on the part of the ministry proved that his conjecture was well founded. Welshmen were too much in earnest over this question to trifle, or toler- ate trifling, with it.

THE RAILWAY HATES AGITATION.

--- The Lancashire and Cheshire Conference on Railway Rates and Charges met in London on Wednysday to confer with members of Parliament, of whom a number were present. :sir J. J. Harwood, who presided. said the Conference was beginning a great crusade with new aspects and new preparations. It was their contention that the railway companies ought to have fixed rates, and in the endeavour to secure these rates the Confer- ence would not be deterred by menaces, from whatever quarter they might come. The followiric resolution was moved by the Mayor of Manchester, and after being supported by many ot the members of Parliament pre- sent, was carried unanimously :—" That this Confer- ence learns with satisfaction that the action taken by Sir James Whitehead and other members of Parliament has already resulted in a very important statement on behalf of her Majesty's Government. At the same time the Conference trusts that members of Parliament will not be satisfied with the intimation recently made by the President of the Board of Trade as a final settlement of the question, but that they will obtain a definite assurance of the intention of the Government to bring in a measure to prevent a repetition of the recent action of the railway companies, and which will relieve traders from any apprehension on the subject in future." If was further resolved to forward a copy of the resolution to the Prime Minister and the President of tha Board of Trade.
