THE RECTOR OF WARRINGTON ON CON G REGATIONALISII.

On Sunday evening the Rector commenced at the Parish Church, Warrington, the first ot a series of addresses on the six principal founts of dissent. There was a good congregation. The Rector said that the forms of dissent which he proposed to deal with were the Independents, or Congregationalists, the Romanists, the Baptists, the Quakers, the Uni- tarians, and the, Wesleyans. The first two separated from the Church in the 18th century ; the next two in the 17th century, and the last two in the 18th century. He asked them to note the billowing point very particu- larly, as it was very instructive. The first sects separ- ated from the Church on the question of discipline, the next two separated on the question of ritual, and the last two on the question of doctrine. As Independents or Congregationalists arose from Presbyterianism and Puritanism he proposed to deal with the history of Presbyterianism, the history of Puritanism, and, thirdly he proposed to give an outline of the teaching of the Independents and Congregationalists. From one point of view the Presbyterians were at one with the Puritans, and yet there was a great difference between the two. The name Presbyterian seemed to point mere to doctrine, and the word Puritan to morality. After alluding to the development of Congregationa ism the Rector said the teaching of the Congregationalists with regard to the Church was that the Church was an invisible body consisting of God's elect scattered throng- out the land, and that there was also a visible Church consisting of those who possessed the true religion. Their teaching from that was that it was wrong to have a high organisation ; that there should not be a high organization of the Church. The teaching of the Church,which was believed to be that of the Apostles, was that the Church was a vis;ble as well as a spiritual society,consisting of the whole company of the baptised. Holy baptism was the gate by winch entrance was ob- tained int@ Christ's Church on earth. Christ was willing that a I should come by baptism into that society, and having come into it he wanted them to remain there and keep together. They believed that Christ appointed ministers and gave them power to appoint their succes- sors,whose successors they were at the present day. For that body he provided means of grace which,if they were only used aright, would be found all that was ne- cessary for the salvation of their souls. The Congrega- tionalists held that each congregation was in a sense a church, and each had power to settle its own doctrine, ritual, and discipline. He asked his hearers to note the fact particularly that the whole of the body had not the settling of religious questions, but the church member- ship of each church settled difficulties according to its own dictates.' The Rector next dealt with the church mem- bership of the Congregationalists. He said that when anyone wanted to be admitted into the inner circle, he had to be subjected to a careful examination as to his experience. What was the result ot that system? It had. been admitted by some of their own body btoßhdeoywwtolcirsaouttl.idtthwonaeye. a very dangerous system, for it excluded some of the s bersse,eatnicli a tincluded hst obmee o couldbest m rdeearn'iy against hypocrisy by such a system as that r Their third point was disbelief in sectari n control and nationaltroi iin thiendowments.ngs secular t be The h iht br cu ht a i theire vaed 6 "National" had never been given by property called Church ; it was given to the Church in times Stantee t he bo} the those who belonged to it. They believed that it was right that the Church should have such property. The Church looked upon it as a trust. To deal with the last point, he said that in 1440 Wycliffe, and in 1550 Calvin held that there were only two orders of ministers —the bishops or presbyters, and deacons. To that teaching the bishops, adhered at the present day. The Congregationalists bele that there were no real sacraments. They observed holy baptiem and holy communion as ordinances and commands of the Lord, but they did not blolt on them as blessed means of grace. They did not hold that in baptism the grace of regene- ration was conferred, nor did they believe that in Holy Communion there was the Body and Blond cf Jesus Christ, as the Church did. The Congregationalists thought much of what they called ordivat ion, but that eniy consisted in the election by the laity accompanied by the laying on of hands, but they did not believe, as the Church did, in the grace conferred by the laying on of hands, nor did they believe in the the grace given 'through the sacraments and sacramental ordinances.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY. FEBRUARY 25, 1893.

THE old watchword" all is well" The Political Situation. may be used with much appropri- ateness when speaking of the 151'esent position, and so far as can be foreseen, of the future prospects of the Government. The PUblication of the full text of the Home Rule Bill has strengthened and extended the favourable °Pinions which were formed of the scheme as ex- Pounded by Mr. GLADSTONE. Of this we may now oe eertain,the Ministry will not be embarrassed by 4issensions amongst their own followers. It is Possible that Mr. GLADSTONE may, in deference to friendly counsel and advice, see fit to modify some of the details ef his great measure, but upon all Points that are vital to the Bill and to the contin- nenee of the the Government in power, he will be able to rely upon the hearty and unanimous eunport of his followers. In the constituencies teo, the Government are faring vastly better than (lid their predecessors in office. The winning of three seats, within a week in districts so different in character and so far apart as Walsall, Ponte- 4act and Hexham, are achievements with which they may well be satisfied, and which will most undoubtedly strengthen their hands in dealing 1 with the obstructions of tm exasperated and baffled °PPosition. The Home Rule Bill having been read a first time, the Government are utilising the interval,which will elapse before the debate on the second reading begins, to push forward some of the reforms that are included in the New- eas_ tle programme. On Monday night, Mr. 11.0vaER, the president of the Local Gov- ernment Board, 'introduced the Registra- !ion Bill in a speech. which was, in I,tB waY, a model of lucid exposition; and Mr. -4-sqtarn on the same evening laid before Parlia- Ment his Employers Liability Bill. The object of

the Government scheme of registration is to make it easy for a person entitled to vote to be put in Possession of the franchise, and therefore, of course, to sweep away the vexatious restrictions and technicalities which now exist, and which, it would almost seem, had been purposely devised to disfranchise as many people as possible. When Mr. FOWLER'S Bill becomes lavy no person will be disqualified from voting because his rates have not 'Wen paid. The duty of seeing that rates are paid will be left to the proper authorities--the rate collectors. Another very necessary reform which the Bill proposes to effect is that of shortening the qualifying period from twelve months to three months. As an argument in favour of this change Mr. FOWLER instanced the deprival of the rights of citizenship which Wesleyan ministers often suffer from, owing to the law as it now is. These ministers usually change their circuit once in three years, and their time for moving is the month of September. If a minister had acquired a fresh qualification in September, 1890, lie would not be able to vote at the General Election in 1892. In July, 1891, he could not have sent in a cla;m. The next year he could do so, but as a matter of fact, he would not be entitled to vote till January, 1893. The law, therefore, which nominally recognises twelve months as the qualify- ing period, really makes eighteen months the minimum, and twenty-seven months the maxi- mum. This will be changed in the future. Another essential part of Mr. FOWLER'S scheme is the pro- posal to place the responsibility of seeing that all who are duly qualified are on the lists,upon officials appointed for the purpose and paid by the County Councils and the Town Councils. In addition to this, the Parliamentary register is to come into force earlier than heretofore, that is to say, on the Ist of November, which is the date at which the municipal register comes into operation. The Employers Liability Bill,of which the Home Secre- tary has charge, is also a very useful Bill, and will further extend the rights of workingmen. Its main objects, as explain° d by Mr.AsouiTu,are the abolition of the doctrine of common employment, to prevent the liability of employers being des- troyed by contract, and to simplify and cheapen the mode of recovering damages.

The Price MR. MUNDELLA and Mr. HOWARD of VINCENT, M.P., have waged an Bread. epistolary battle over the price of the four-pound loaf. The encounter has been a brief one and it has ended entirely in Mr. MUN- DELLA'S favour. Mr. HOWARD VINCENT, as every one knows, is one of the victims of the Protection- ist craze, and. he is extremely anxious to make it appear that bread is dear in England, that it is, at all events, dearer in England than in Protec- tionist countries. One of the strange and absurd delusions which the advocates of Protection are anxious to foster is, that because wheat and flour are untaxed and cheap,therefore bread is dear. Mr. HOWARD VINCENT alleges that the French artisan or peasant, buys his bread for a less prico than does the British workingman, but in order to give some plausibility to this contention he boldly as- serts that the four-pound loaf is sold in England at the present time at sevenpence halfpenny. Now, when a Protectionist makes an assertion which is capable of proof or disproof he is almost sure to be found out, and, as the saying is, "hoist with his own petard." It is so in this case, for Mr. MUNDELLA at once informs Mr. HOWARD VINCENT that except in the aristocratic locality of Gros- venor-square and similar fashionable quarters, where fancy bread is used, no such price as seven- pence halfpenny is paid for the four-pound loaf. From inquiries made he has ascertained that in the North of Ireland and the North of England the price is from four-pence half-penny to five- pence half-penny; in Staffordshire a penny a pound, and in different parts of London about five-pence half-penny the loaf. In some cases the quotations are less than any of the prices named, whilst bread of even the very finest quality is not charged at m ore than sixpence half-penny the four-pound loaf. From personal inquiries in France Mr. MUNDELLA ascertained that bread there was dearer than in England, and, as for quality, it had about it a colour and texture which would be "objected to by our English workmen and even by the residents in our union workhouses." Then, as to Italy, another Protec- tionist country, a Foreign Office report showed that in December last the prices of the four-pound loaf ranged from sevenpence in Milan to ninepence half-penny in Rome. "You will thus perceive," says Mr. MUNDELLA in a sentence which is well worth pondering," that in Protectionist countries the price of bread is from twenty-five to fifty per cent dearer than it is in this country."

What Mr, ASQUITH aptly

Welsh Disestablishment. described as the first step towards the disestablishment and disendowment of the Welsh Church was taken in the House of Commons on Thursday night. This was the introduction and the first reading, by a majority of fifty-six, of the Welsh Suspensory Bill. It is true that this Bill itself, when made into law,will neither disestablish nor disendow the Welsh Church. It is simply a preliminary to dis- establishment and disendowraent, and it is a I measure similar in its provision to one that was passed before the Irish Church was dealt with. It has about it none of the laboured verbosity and tortuous phraseology which are often associated with Acts of Parliament, for it consists of one single clause providing that in all appointments made after the passing of the Bill the emoluments of the office shall be held by the new incumbent subject to Che pleasure of Parliament. The effect of this will be to prevent new claims being estab- lished for compensation when the final measure of disestablishment is enacted. The speech in which Mr. ASQUITH introduced and explained the Bill will very materially enhance his Parliamentary reputation. It has been described as a " model of Ministerial brevity" for it scarcely exceeded half an hour. He certainly amply justi- fied the policy of the Government with regard to Welsh disestablishment. He was sarcastically effective in dealing with the " belated controver- • sialists " who have no arguments, but who make up for the dearth of them by the loudness of their , outcries about what they are pleased to call " plunder" and " sacrilege." As long as the Irish Disestablishment Act stood unropealed, Mr. Asourrn contended, amidst the approving ap- plause of the Ministerialists, that such epithets as these should be relegated to some musaum of poli- tical antiquities. That the Welsh themselves demand Disestablishment, is amply proved by the fact that, out of thirty-four members returned at the General Election, thirty-one were pledged to it. The average majority of these thirty-one members was no less than two thousand, while, of the three members who are opposed to Disestab- lishment, the majority of one was only ninety- eight, and that of the other, one hundred and

DEATH OF A RUNCORN TRADESMAN.

On Thursday afternoon there was interred in the family grave at Warburton Church, the mortal remains of Mr. Wrn. Broadhurst, grocer of High-st., Runcorn, who died after a brief illness on Sunday afternoon last. The deceased gentleman who had attained the age of 63 years, had been engaged in business in the town for more than 20 years. Although not a " public man "he was widely known and respected, and much sympathy was expressed for the widow and four children in their great sorrow. How THE Poort RATE IS MADE UP. On Thursday at the Widnes Petty Sessions, Patrick Doyle, Lugsdale-road, was charged with deserting his wife, who had been chargeable to the common fund of the Preseot Union, since 10th Sept., 1891. The cost to the Guardians had been £l2 4s.—The defendant said he could not live with his wife on account of her intemper- ate habits. If he took a house he would not live long, brcause of the glasses and jugs that would be thrown at him. He was willing to keep his wife in the Workhouse, hut he would not take a house. He was sent to gaol for 14 days' bard labour. If you wantyour watch repaired by skilful workmen. take it to PRITCHARD'S.—Advt.

Two MONTIIS_ FOR NOT SUPPORTING A WIFE,

On Wednesday at Runcorn Police Court before Air. 11. Reynolds and Mr. G. L. Davies a labourer named Edward James Waterson, of Mersey-road Widnes, was brought up on a warrant charged with non-compliance with a maintenance order made by the magistrates for the sup- port of his wife and two children. Although it is twelve months since the order was made, the prisoner had not , made one contribution, and he had served two terms of imprisonment in consequence. The prisoner pleaded that he could not do any work because of an injury he received three years ago, but the Bench declined to accept this explanation, and he was sentenced to two months hard labour. JOSEPH PRITCHARD is prepared to wind and keep in repair. private clocks from 10s. per annum.—Advt. SEPARATION BEFORE 11011 TE AND RELATION'S. Before Mr. J. Brock and Mr. J. Stone, at Widnes Petty Sessions on Thursday, James Duxbury, of Padiham, was summoned by his wife for £1 55., arrears of a mainten- ance order made on the twelfth of January last.—The complainant stated that her husband had only paid the costs of the order.—ln reply to Mr. F. J. Poole' who appeared for the defendant, the complainant said she knew that her husband was working for Colonel Starkie at Padiham, and had a house rent free. and he had offered her a home there. She refused to do se because she could not live with his relations, but she had written to him to say that she might live with him again if be came to reside in Widnes.—The defendant said he would rather have a distress warrant issued than go to gaol, and the warrant was issued. The Bench Wormed the defendant that if lie had not sufficient goods to satisfy the warrant he would have to go to gaol for 21 days. SUCCESS OF A RUNCORN FIRM. We are informed that Messrs. Timmins, of Runcorn have received instructions from the Gainsborough Local Beard, Lincolnshire, to deepen their waterworks artesian well. The firm executed this work some years ago. The well commenced 24 inches diameter and terminated 10i inches diameter at 1,202 feet from SUIafpRCH. It is now intended to deepen it several hundred feet, with the object of increasing the yield of water from 18,000 gal- lons to 26,000 gallons per hour. The to of Gains- borough was formerly supplied from the river Trent, and there was a great outcry about the waste of money in boring for water. This prejudice has been entirely overcome, and the inhabitants now prefer the under- ground water. Messrs. Timmins have also important :water contracts in hand for the Dukes of Westminster and Portland : thee have also been consulted by the :Preston Rural Sanitary authority, and have reported on the supply of water to their rural district of 80/118 30 ' sqw,re mile. PRITCHARD'S have now stood the teat for 50 years, and quality and tint-class work manship are still guaranteed.—Adet. ALLEGED STABBING BY A WOMAN AT WIDNES.

On Monday, at Widnes Police Court, before Mr. H. Wade Deacon, a woman named Elizabeth Bushell, Edmund-street, was charged with wounding Edmund Macdermott. The evidence showed that on Saturday afternoon the prisoner and Macdermott were drinking together in one of the upper rooms of prosecutor's house Macdermott returned home about six o'clock in the evening, and prisoner asked him to drink with them. He did so, and subsequently Bushell asked him to pay for a pint of beer, but lie refused, saying he had no money. Prisoner then became most violent, picked up a bottle containg hot water, threw it at him, and then took a knife of the table and stabbed Macdermott on the head with it, inflicting a dangerous wound, which penetrated to the inner t'bone of the skull. Dr. Allen, who attended the injured man, said it would be some titne before he would be able to appear in court, and prisonor was remanded until Monday next. JOSEPH PRITCHARD. Watchmaker and Jeweller, 51, High- et.. and 4. Bridge-st., Runcorn.—Advt. How TO REACH TIM MASSES. For the elucidation of this question a public meeting in connection with the combined parishes of Farnworth Widnes, and St. Ambrose, was held in St. Paul's Paro- chial Room on Thursday night. The proceedings were under the auspices of the Church Pastoral Aid Society. There was a fair attendance. The chair was occupied by Mr. T. S. Timmis, J.P., who in his opening address said the Church had many noble objects in view but none more important than to bring the true religion home to the masses of the country. The members who attended Church were not what they ought to be, but in Widnes Church matters were looking up. Twenty-seven years ago when he first came to Widnes, there was only one small Church and no organization, now they had four churches with energetic ministers, all pulling together, aided by able c urates. He had taken a great interest in Church work and would continue to do so. The Rev. W. Cain delivered an address and said the masses who were living in sin were tobe reached by the Gospel, and he advocated the claims of the Church Pastoral Aid Society for their support in consequence of the work it was accomplishing in this direction. The Rev. I. T. Madden, Rev. G. H. Garrett, J. Wright Wil- liams and bthers addressed the meeting, dwelling upon the saving powers of the scriptures properly brought home to the people. TITINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

RUNCORN

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDRNT.

Within the last few days great complaints hay.e been made of the Water Works Company increasing their charges. The rate, I understand, is now charged on the rental instead of the rateable value, and this alteration is made under the autlh,rity of the following clause of the Water Works Act—" The Company shall at the request of the owner or occu- pier of any private dwelling •house, or part of a private dwelling house, in any street in which any pipe of the Company shall be laid, or on the application of any person. who, under the pro- visions of this Act, oi any Act incorporated here- with, shall be entitled to demand a supply of water for domestic purposes furnished to such owner or occupier or other person, a sufficient supply of water for domestic uses, at rates not exceeding the yearly rates herein specified according to the an- nual rack rent or value of such dwelling house or part of a dwelling house; (that is to say) : Where the annual rock rent or value of the premises so supplied with water shall not exceed £2O at the rate per centum per annum not exceeding six pounds; not exceeding £4O, 5i per cent. per annum ; not exceeding £BO, five per cent. per annum ; and exceeding £BO, 41 per cent., per annum. For water closets, the charges are limited to 3s. per year for . one water closet in a dwelling house of a yearly value not exceeding £2O ; 4s. per year for one in a house not exceeding £4o,an I ss. per year for one in a house exceeding £4O. For every bath, the Company are entitled to charge the sum of 10s. per annum. Provided always Oat the Company shall not be obliged to furnish any supply of water for any less sum than 2d. per week."— Here is a local subject, the newly formed Liberal Association or the Rate- payers Association may appropriately take up, and do the ratepayers a service by testing the legality I of the increased charge for water.

The Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority have dealt with the smallpox outbreak with commen4l- - determination and promptitude, but the ex- pense is likely to become very heavy in so scattered IL district as the Autli ority rule. To so rigid an economist as Mr. James Parr,t his expenditure must be harrowing to his soul, and he hit upon a novel idea on Tuesday for lessening the burden on the ratepayers. He desired a "nice letter" to be sent to Lord Egerton,appealing fortinancial help in their difficulty, but the majority of the members would not take the matter seriously, and it was hinted to Mr. Parr that as he was well endowed with this , world's goods, and had sold a nice slice of land to the Ship Canal Co., he might come forward with a ' generous contribution himself, and so help his less fortunate brethren. This view of the case did not commend itself to Mr. Parr, and eventually his mo- tion was practically " laughed out."

If the Runcorn Water Bill does nothing else, it has done one good thing in creating great, interest amongst the ratepayers wit It regard to the doinszs of the Board of Commissioners. With a Liberal. Association and a Ratepayers Association keeping a critical eye on local affairs, with a view to action our local rulers may look out for squalls if they chance to run counter to the wishes of the members of these two bodies. Former ratepayers associa- tions have been weak and short lived organizations but perhaps the present one may be of sturdier build and have a longer and more vigorous existence

The Liberal Association performed one excellent duty at its first meeting on Wednesday night in requesting Mr.Brunner, M.P., to move that Boards of Commissir,ners shall be included in the Local Authorities (Voting and Qualification) Bill. This Bill practically confers one man one vote, and the ballot upon Local Boards and Boards of Guardians, and those who have any experience with the elec- tion of Runcorn Commissioners will agree with Mr. Speakman that the present system is a disgraceful one, and ought to be altered.

Runcorn Board uf Guardians has passed a resoln- tion in favour of taxing bicycles and tricycles. It the members had read the Chancellor of the Exche- quer's letter on that subject they would have known how futile is such a resolution.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUATtDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. J. Mclntyre, was the chairman. In addition to the members present at the meeting of the Rural Sanitary Authority there were in attendance the Rev. Dr. Gesteldi, Messrs. Samuel ' Taylor, T. Lea and A. R. Norman, Mr. J. Ashton (clerk), and Mr. G. Ashton (aeputy clerk). LAND AND ITS TAXATION. A letter from the Sleaford Union was read asking for the Board's approval and support of the following reso- lution :—That the present depressed condition of agri- culture calls for immediate relief of the land from the existirr, unjust method of collecting the poor rate on the Union Parochial Assessment and this Board of Guardians recognising the fact that the maintenance of the destitute poor should be a national and not a local charge, urges upon the Government the justice and necessity of so amending section 20 to 26 of the Local Goverument Act, 1688, as to substitute the repayment of three-fourths of the Union common charges, as audited by the Govern- ment auditor, instead of only the payment of the salaries of union officers as at present ordered by that Act, and that a copy of this resolution be forwarded to the Prime Minister, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, the President of the Local Government Board, the Minister of Agricul- ture, the members of the division and to every Union in England and Wales.—The letter was laid on the table.

WIDNES SCHOOL BOARD.

DISCUSSION ON COTRACTS.

MUNIFICENT GIFTS TO WIDNES.

The monthly meeting of this Board was held on Tuesday afternoon in the Town Hall. There were present :—Mr. H. Wade Deacon, (in the chair),Revs. J. Clarke, Dean Finnegan, J. H. Garratt, Dr. O'Keeffe, and Messrs. S. Quinn, C. Flynn, C. E. Barlow, R. Shaw, D. Lewis, and G. H. Danby (clerk.)

GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE. This Committee reported upon Mr. Timms' gift of £2,500 for the establishment of a grant to assist capable boys in obtaining a higher education, and recom- mended :—" (a) That the deed of trust establishing the Timmis Fund and the Timmis Grants be approved, and that the seal of the Board be affixed thereto, and that the chairman and vice-chairman be authorised to sign the same on behalf of the board ; (b) that, in accept- ing the gift, the Board begs to express its high appre- ciation of the generosity of Mr. T. Sutton Timmis, and its admiration for thepublic spirit he he has inavariably displayed."—Dr. O'Keeffr moved the adoption cf these recommendations, and, in doing so, paid a tribute to Mr. Timmis for the great interest he had always taken in i educational matters in the town.—The Chairman, n seconding the adoption of the minutes, said that after one or two alterations had been made in the draft deed submitted by Mr. Timmis, the committee had unanim- ouslvdecided to submit it to the Board for their approval. Mr.Tinunis had been the Chairman of that Board for some- thing like twelve years, and during that time he showed the greatest amount of interest in educational work in the district,and it was only iii keeping with his previous generosity that when he resigned the chairmanship of the Board he made some signal mark of benefit to ele- mentary education in the town.—Mr. D. Lewis sup- ported the motion, and the recommendations were passed.

THE TAXING OF CYCLES. -•- • -

A communicalrol was submitted from the Wirral Highway Board, with regard to the taxation and regul- ation of bicycles and tricycles used on public highways. The Board considered that the time had arrived when an effort should be made to compel all persons using the public highways on any kind of carriage to contri- bute towards their repair. They suggested that the Runcorn Board of Guardians should use their influence with the members of Parliament with a view to the matter being brought before the House of Commons.— On the motion of Mr. W. Whitlow, seconded by Mr. J. Weir,a resolution was passed in favour of taxing bicycles and tricycles and the Clerk was instructed to forward it to the member of Parliament for the Division.

SITES AND BUILDING COMMITTEE. This Committee recommended "That all contractors, in carrying out contracts for this committee, shall pay the rate of wages and observe the hours of labour recog- nised and agreed upon between the trades unions and employers respectively in the district of Widnes, or such rate of wages and hours as are equivalent and approxi- mate thereto, and that contractors shall not transfer, assign, or underlet. directly or indirectly, any contract, or any part share or interest therein, without the written consent of the Widnes School Board, under the hand of the clerk."—Mr. Quinn moved the adop- tion of the minutes, and the Chairman seconded the re- comniendation.—Mr. Barlow was strongly opposed to the recommendation, because he thought it an unduo and unnecessary interference with other people's business.—Mr. Shaw said he was opposed to the recom- inendation,:.nd thought it was only a pandering to a fool- ish idea. He did not like that. He believed in justice,and he did not know one employer of labour in Widnes who had done work for that Board who ever attempted or had any desire to act unjustly to his workmen. There was not the slightet need for the recommendation, and he hoped that both the mover and seconder would be willing to withdraw it.—The Rev. Father Clarke said they had facts before them which showed that some of their contractors had employed unprofessional men at a lower rate of wages to do work.—Dean Finegan and Mr. C. Fiynn, whilst both agreeing with the first portion of the motion, said they could not support the latter por- tion. as they thought the board had no right to interfere in the subletting of contracts. If they (lid, they would have a very great difficulty in letting their contracts.— The Chairman, in reply, said the motion was a similar one to that passed by their own Corporation, and he had not heard any complaints about the way it worked. The London County Council, the London School Board, and other public bodies had also passed similar resolu- tions. After a protracted discussion, the recommenda- tion was confirmed by 6 votes to 2, two members ab- staining.

ALLEGRD MURDEROUS ASSAULT.—On Wednesday at the Frodsham Petty ,Sessions, a navvy, named George Davies, applied to the magistrates (Messrs. C. Reynolds and A. Given away with a pound of tea," when the gift consists of a coupon, brings the the transaction within the scope 0 the Lottery Act. On Monday, at Cannock, a travelling auctioneer named , Pearson was deed £5 and costs in respect of three ' offences under that Act. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

The IV arrington Tories are a " rum lot." By this I don't infer anything "spirituous " but certainly their sayings one day and their actions the next don't always exactly tally. A Radical musn't "look over a hedge," but a Tory may" steal a sheep "and its all right.

A few short weeks ago, the Tories were all "gibes and floutsand jeers" at the poverty stricken Radicals who only managed to raise .£l5O as a testimonial to a man of a hora Warrington was not worthy (Dr. Houston). They (the Tories) next tried their hands to raise a testimonial to Sir Gilbert Greenall and appealed especially to the "Conserva- tive workingman "for help. Now, sir, the Conser- vative workingman is either a diminishing quantity, or else he is very poor, or else he is very mean. All told, the Tory workingmen, the Tory shopkeepers, the Tory professional men and the Tory aristocracy in Warrington, have only raised .£l2O for the purpose in question. After all the parade of ward Committees—with a real Tory workingman on each —special workingmen canvas- sers and the like, the efforts of thP Tory mountain in labour have produced this wietchod little mouse.

Well, after all the Tory Councillors and others engaged upon the highly creditable mission of button-holing all and sundry in their universal touting for " coppers "for the " Baronet "of Wal- ton, had exhausted their efforts, they met together, totted all up,and finding the sum total so much be- low their expectations that they were ashamed to let it be known they decided to make a further appeal to to the" fobs' of the party to " make it out," When all was done that could be thought of, the Committee met again to decide what to buy, and where to buy it. Of course the " Equestrian Statue "idea of Alderman Francomb had long since vanished. I should think the worthy Alderman has regretted that silly notion many a time. The " Bust " notion was also impiacticable because the cash wouldn't run to it ; so ultimately a "Plate" for Sir Gilbert and a " Star "for Lady ultimately were fixed upon. Of course, £l2O is amply sufficient to purchame " Plate" and a " Star" of a quality in ac- cord with the surroundings of a millionaire and his lady!

When I heard what had been decided upon, lat once concluded that our old friend Mr. Alfred Woods was bound to get the order for the lot. lam not particularly an admirer of Mr. Alfred Woods in some things,hut I am bound to say that as a trades man he is capable and honourable and as a worke— in the Tory Cause he has been ardent and enthus i astic for very many years. Ile fought two very ho- municipal battles for his party, when everybody knew he was fighting a forlorn hope and when no other Tory could be got to tackle the job and in a large measure the ill-health from which I regret he has suffered so severely, and so long, is attributable to over-exertions in the cause of his party.

Well! Mr. Alfred Woods has ,wt got this large order, and strange to say, neither has any other Warrington tradesman obtained it. The Testimonial Committee have totally ignored the claims of Mr. Alfred Woods upon his party,have treated with con- tempt the notion that money collected in Warring- ton might reasonably be spent in Warrington, and have passed the order on direct to Messrs. Liking- ton, the celebrated silversmiths. Talk about a snub? This is a snub with a vengeance ! lam really and truly very sorry for Mr. Woods, and I did think after this exhibition of callous indiffe- rence to the interosts of Warrington tradesmen (with whom of late, owing to the health of the town, business has been far from • bright) on the part of the Testimonial Committee, the people who have subscribed the £l2O will not be particularly happy.

I hear that the various Rural Authorities, whose districts touch the Sankey Brook,together with the Warrington Corporation, are about to devise a scheme for preventing the further pollution of that flowing disgrace of chemical matter, miscellaneous filth, and obnoxious odours, which, for variety " knock " anything from a Billingsgate Market to a Longford Depot. It is desirable that these authori- ties should take this step, for if there is a more pitiable instance of the ruthless dsetruction of a once picturesque, clear, and prolific fishing stream, it is not in the neighbourhood of Warrington. At present the brook is a nuisance to residents in its proximity, whereas if its pollution were ended, it would prove a pretty and useful neighbour. While on this subject, I should like to Know when is the prevention of the pollution of the Mersey to take effect ? _

MERLIN.

RUNCORN VOLUNTEER SHOOTING CLUB.

- - The annual meeting for the election of officers and other business in connection with the above club, took place on Wednesday evening, at Mr. A. Riley's café. An excellent dinner was served, and after the removal of the cloth Captain D. Ashton took the chair. He was supported by Captain H. Harrison. of I Company, Frodshatn ; Captain G. Ashton, and Lieu- tenant E. Speakman. Sergeant E. W. Woodland read a brief report, showing that in the friendly matches that had taken place during the past season, victory had on each occasion, crowned the efforts of the Runcorn team. The financial statement, was equally satisfactory, as it showed that the membership was increased by 13 entries and a balance of over £2 was left to be disposed of. A resolution approving of the report and balance sheet was carried, as was also a resolution that a portion of the balance be spent in connection with the annual dinner then being held. Major C. W. Hazlehurst was re-elect- ed president of the club, the Commissioned officers of Runcorn detachment, with Captain Harrison of 1 Com- pany, Quartermaster Elankinson, and /fir. A. Timmins, were elected vice-president. The some committee of non-commissioned officers and privates as last year ; and Sergeant J. A. Rushton and Sergeant J. W. Woodland were respectively re-elected treasurer and secretary. At the close of the business the meeting resolved itself into one of a harmonious character. The usual loyal and patriotic toasts were duly honoured. The enjoyment of the evening was considerably enhanced by the presenne of Mr. Lancaster (who presided at the pianoforte). Mr. Branch, Mr. J. Nickson, and Mr. Bushell, who cpntri- bated a selection of music.

SHOCKING DEATH FROM EXPOSURE AT RUNCORN.

Yesterday. Mr. J. E. Worsley. coroner, held an in- quest at the Cemetery Offices, Runcorn, on the body of Mary Berry, a vagrant character, who was found in a dying condition in Moughland-lane, Higher Runcorn, on Thursday morning, and died a few minutes later.— James Worrell, said he lived on Runcorn Common, and was cowman to Mr. Pea- cock. On Thursday morning at ten minutes to six o'clook he found the deceased lying on her back under the bridge in Moughland-lane. Her cheat was bare, and her clothes were all disarranged. He got hold,of her and shouted but she gave no answer. A short distance along the road, he met P.C. Ben- field and he told the officer.—P.C. Bentield deposed that he found the woman as described. There was not the slightest evidence of a struggle haring taken place. She was quite unconscious. With the assistance of Worrall he carried her to Mr. Ackerley's farm, and administered a cup of hot tea, and placed hot water bottles to her feet. He also tried artificial respir- ation. She never regained consciousness, and died in ten minutes. There was no marks of violence on the body. Rethought the woman had died from exposure. —Supt. Atherton said he saw the deceased twice on Wednesday. She was drunk. Ele had made inquiries and found that she discharged herself from the Work- house on Wednesday morning. James Savage, was charged on a war- rant, on Friday, with sot having appeared to a sum- mons for assaulting his wife on August 18, 1891.—Mr. P. Davies appeared for the defendant.--=Mrs. Savage said tint on the above date, her husband had only given her fis. for the week. She went to a public-house to bring him home, and on the way home he abused her scandal- ously. He kicked her about the body and bruised her and took her umbrella off her and broke ft over his knee. When prisoner got home he locked her out. He was summoned the following week, but had left the town.— Mrs. Bromley corroborated the plaintiff's statement as to the bruises on her body.—P.C. Barlow said that on Thursday night, he was in Foundry-st., and there met prisoner. tie at once arrested him and took him to the police station.—Mr. Davies said that his client told him that he worked late on the night of the allOged assault, and went to a public-house. He was annoyed at his wife coming after him, and alleged that she hit him with her umbrella. He took it off her and broke it. He denied com:nitting the assault. The magistrates said that they were satisfied that an aggravated assault was committed and granted an order for separation, order- ing the husband to pay his wife Bs. per week. THEFT FROM SHOPS BY CHILDREN.:-THE MAGISTRATES

AND MAD/OMEN'S GOODS.

this Corfield, aged 12 years, and Bri..iget Dolan, aged 10 years were each chargel with stealing a pair of Dec/fa, valued 5 111, from the shot, door of Stead and Simpson, Horsemarket-st., on the 18th inst., and Dolan was also charged with stealing a pair of girl's boots from Joseph Frisby, in Market Place, on the 11th inst. Mr. Hunt (Chief Constable), said that Corfield was the daughter of Patrick Corfield, Pump Yard, off Golborne- st„and Dolan resided with her grandmother in Foundry- it. On Saturday night, the boots were missing from Stead and Siren:tons shop, and the police were at once informed. A sample boot was given and on the follow- ing Monday the boots were discovered at Tunstall's pawnshop, where they had been pledged by the younger prisoner. Dolan was arrested by Detective Monaghan. She was brought down to the Police Station, and she then said that Corfield had given her the shoes. Carfield was then brought to the Police Station, and when the prisoners were brought together, Dolan said that Cor- field had rubbed the bottoms of the boots in an entry to dirty them. Corfield then charged Dolan with stealing a pair of boots from Frisby's, apd, on inquiry, these boots were also found at Tunstall's. Thomas Smart Kent, manager of Stead and Simpsons identified the boots, and Edith Holbrook, assistant in Tunstall's pawn- shop, identified Dolan as the one who had pledged the boots. Mr. Garnett said that this was a difficult case to deal with. Last week he saw some remarks in the papers on the question of shopkeepeis. He had a strong feeling that shopkeepers ought to take special care to see that their goods were properly protected, in order to prevent peculation of this sort. Shepkeepers were to some extent t 3 blame for 4i ng temptation in the way of persons who were um bl to resist it. More care should be taken. The writer of those remarks had re- ferred to the Market, but in the market there was always someone to look after a stall, while in the shop, if the assistants were engaged behind the counter, they Could not see outside, and thus there was too great a temptation for some people to resist. In some towns netting was placed round the outside goods, and he thought means should be taken to exhibit the goods so that they could not be taken away. Mr. Kent said that the goods at the outside of the shop were connected with an alarm inside so that they conld know the instant any of them were taken. The boots taken by the pri- soners were in the doorway, and a boy had been em- ployed that day to give out bills, and to look after the outside of the shop. If the goods were not well exhib- ited, it would be impossible for proprietors to make their way. The prisoner Corfield for this offence was fined 103., and Dolan was ordered to be sent to gaol for 14 days, so that she might be sent to a reformatory for five years.

THR FRIO?! OF MILE. Mr. MUIR asked how it was that 2s. 3d. was paid in the house, and 2s. 4d. in the schools for milk.—The CHAIRMAN explained that the milk had to be brought by the inmates to the house, but it was taken to the schools.

_ .. ROUSE COMMITTEH.—A SURPRISE VISIT. SCHOOL COMMITTEE. 'lt was resolved to have an outside staircase to No.l home in Pad gate schools, and also to heat the home, by means of hot water pipes.—The doctor reported that the children were healthy.—The CLERK said that it would be necessary to submit the proposed improvements to the Local Government Board, as they were structural alter- ations.—Mr. DOBSON thought it was a queer idea to put pip's in the school now winter was over.—The recom- mendation of the School Committee were passed unani- mously.

FINANCE.

The CLERK announced that the county rate from the Board amounted to £2,276 17s. 9d. The audit for the last half-year would be held in the Council room of the Town Hall on March 13.

- - PATIENTS IN BLIND ASYLUMS. Mr. SaAw said he should like to say a few words in regard to patients in blind asylums. He thought it only right that so long as the Board had patients in these asylums, they should have a committee to pay periodical visits, such as they did to lunatic asylums. The Board had two patients, a man and a boy, in a blind asylum at Manchester. There ought to be some kind of visitation to these patients so long as they had them.—The CLERK suggested that Mr. Shaw should bring the question before the House Committee. He did not know how the authorities of these institutions arranged for visitois. —Mr. RAT CLIFFE wished the Clerk to write to them for information. ELECTION OF RELIEVING OFFICER. The next business was to elect a successor to Mr. Wallace relieving officer of No. 2 division. The CLERK said that as, during the past year, the Board had twice advertised for vacancies, and had twice reduced the number of the applicants to a select number, it was considered sufficient that these persons some of whom were so nearly appointed before, should be invited to present themselves again, as candidates. 'rhere were therefore seven candidates for the situation, viz.,Messrs. F. A. Aukland, G. Clarke, F. Davies, A. Frost, S. H. Jenkins, E J. Mitchell, and F. Wright. The Board then proceeded with the election and the candidates were reduced to two, Messrs. F. A. Aukland, and F. Wright. On a vote being taken, it was found that Mr. F. A. Aukland had polled 14 votes to Mr. F. Wright 12. Mr. F. A. Aukland, therefore, was declared elected, and he will be formally appointed at the next meeting of the Board.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 25, 1893.

pressly declared that the rates of Customs and Excise, as well as those of the Postal Department, were to be fixed by the Imperial Parliament, and being fixed in thnt way for the Irish service of course the Irish members would vote upon them. The right Ii n. gentleortu. in the course of a statement on the business ot the House, said the Government would propose next Monday that they should take first on Monday the introducpion of the Local Veto Bill, and secondly the Employers Liability Bill.

WELSH DISESTABLISHMENT.

Mr. Asiaurrif moved for leave to bring ima bill to pre- vent for a limited time the creation of new interests in Church of England bishoprics, dignities, and benefices in Wales and Monmouthshire. He said the policy of Welsh DisestablishmeLt was in the minds of the electors of the country when they recorded their votes last summer, and Her Majesty's Government would be failing in their duty if they did not take advantage of the first oppor- tunity which offered to give prompt and effectual execu- tion to this policy. Sir J. GORST moved an amendment declaring that it was inexpedient and unjust to interfere by legislation with the operations of the Church in the Welsh dioceses while the principle of an alteration in the relations between Church and State in that part of the United Kingdom had not been adopted by Parliament. Sir J. MOWBRAY seconded the amendment. Mr. RRNDEL stated the case of the Welsh Liberal members in support of the Home Secretary's motion, and said the opposition of the Welsh people was not to the Church but to the Establishment, an excellent example of the mischiev- ous working of which was furnished by the appoint- ment of the Bishop of St. Asaph by Lord Salisbury. Mr. KENYON spoke against the bill. Sir G. 0. Mottoes said the results of the last elections showed that the majority in favour .of disestablishment had grown from 46 per cent, to something like 66 per cent. Lord R. CHURCH! LL said the House was asked to give a pledge in favour of disestablisbmant without any proper inquiry and with no information as to the position of the Church or the policy of the Government. The sole object of the Government in this matter was to secure votes for their Irish policy. Mr. G LADS TONE said that when the noble lord said they endeavoured to play Welsh dises- tablishment against Home Rule Re would plainly reply that he was not ashamed of either the one or the other proposal. On a division the numbers were.—For Mr. Asquith's motion, 301; against ; 2,5; Government majority, 56. Leave was then given to tiring in the bill.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

FRIDAY.

In the House of Commons on evening the ad-1 journed debato on the HOlll5 Rule Rill was resumed by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, who agreed that the retention of the Irish members at Westminster was an essential condition of the maintenanee of existing Parliamentary supremacy and the unity of the empire. He asserted, however, that the policy the House Was now asked to adopt was an irrevocable policy. Mr. Blake, Mr. Courtney, Mr. Ren- ton], and Mr. Goschen followed, the latter declaring that the bill would never pass into law. The bill was after- wards read a first time amidst loud Ministerial cheers.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on -Monday evening Mr. T. W. RUSSELL questioned the Chief Secretary for Ireland about the legality of the Cork City Council incurring the cost of a deputation to Rome to present an address of congratulation to the Pope on the occasion of his jubilee, and elicited the information that all the Con- servative and Protestant members of the Council who were present voted for the expenditure, and that the minority against it was composed entirely of Roman Catholics.

THE QUEENS UNIFORM. In replying to an inquiry about the grievance of cer- tain warrant officers of the army in regard to restric- tions against the wearing of plain clothes. Mr. CAMP- BELL-BANNERMAN said he would give no countenance to the idea that it was a privilege to put off Her Majesty's uniform or an infliction to hAve to wear it, and he was not sure that there was not a good deal to be said in favour of making the wearing of uniform more obliga- tory than it was upon the officer ranks of the army. THE CHURCH OF ENGLAND ABROAD. Mr. BUXTON informed Mr. Carvell Williams that the Colonial Secretary had intimated to the Governor of British Guiana that he considered that the Church of England in that colony already received a full proportion of assistance from public funds, and that he was not prepared to approve of any further vote being proposed.

• • CHAI RMANSHIP OF COMMITTRS. - • -

Mr. Mellor, on the motion of the PRIINIBEt, was ap- pointed Chairman of Committees of the House.

THE REGISTRATION BILL. Mr. FOWLER asked leave to bring in the Registration Bill. He said the objections to the length of the quali- fying period for the residential franchise were obvious. The minimum was 18 months and the maximum two years and three months before a householder was entitled to exercise the franchise. Then the registration machinery was cumbersome, and many persons who ought to be registered lost their votes, whilst others were on the register who ought not to be there ; and the system by which the accuracy of the register depended mainly on the activity of registration agents was objectionable in principle and mischievous in practice. The Government proposed to abolish the con- ditions with reference to rating and to repeal all provi- sions of the registration laws requiring the payment either of assessed rates or poor rates, and they had come to the conclusion that the qualifying period should be re- duced to one uniform period of three months, to end on the 24th June in every year. The registration was to be ' entrusted to a class of officials called superintendent and district registrars, to be applinted by the Town Councils and County Councils. Mr. STANSPIELD said that on the whole the bill was admirably conceived. Mr. E. STAN- HOPE said that a six months' term would be better than three months, and said the Opposition would oppose any attempt to put on the rates any expense for the purpose of performing that which, as they maintained, was the duty of the State. After some further discussion leave was given to introdu,ce the Bill, and the second reading was provisionally fixed for next Monday. Mr. ASQUITH moved the second reading of the Bill 0 amend the

EMPLOYERS LIABILITY ACT,.

and explained that it abolished the doctrine of common employment, Prohibited contracts by the workman which redounced his statutory rights, and simplified the procedure.

In the House of Lords Lord Londonderry called atten- tion to the recent refusal of the Irish Executive to grant police protection to sheriffs executing night distraints. The Lord Chancellor stated that an appeal would be carried to the House of Lords from the decisiod of the Insh Court of Queen's Bench. Lord Salisbury and other Tory peers protested against the acti.n of the Govern- ment, and Lord Kimberley denied that the Executive had attempted to exercise a " dispensing power."

The motion was by leave withdrawn. Answering several questions on the subject of

RAILWAY RAM, Mr MINDSLLA said Sir H. Oakley was afraid the com- panies would not get through the work or revision before the middle of April, but he assured him (Mr. Mundella) that they were most anxious to arrive at a settlement, and were doing all that was possible to meet the reason- able wishes of the traders.

_ AMERICAN EMIGRATION. Mr. MUNDELLA, in reply to Mr. J. Lowther, said the Commissioners to be sent to the United States to inquire into the question of immigration were Mr. Burnett, the Labour Correspondent 01 the Board of Trade, and Mr. David Schloss, barrister-at-law.

TRH 7 QUESTION OF REDISTRIBUTION. Mr. KIMBER called attention to the disparity of repre- sentation existing between certain constituencies of the United Kingdom, and moved a resolution declaring that such disparity should be forthwith inquired into by an impartial committee or commission, and remedied.—Mr. MACARNTSBY seconded the motion.—Sir CHARLES DILE moved an amendment deprecating the reference of the subject to a select committee or royal commission, but expressing the opinion that the discrepancies in electoral power deserved the attention of her Majesty's Govern- ment with a view to legislation in a future session.— Sir W. HARCOURT suggested that having attained his ob- ject which was to bring the subject before public notice, Mr. Kimber should not press the motion. He (Sir WILLIAM) had no doubt that, whatever Government was in power, this question would have to be dealt with be- fore many years were over. Both motion and amend- ment were then withdrawn. Shortly after eight o'clock the House was counted out—for the first time this session.

In the House of Lords, Lord RIBBLESDALE, replying to the Earl of DIINRAV6N, said he believed the Commission on Agricultural Depression would have power to go in- to the incidence of taxation on agricultural land separ- ately if they thought fit.

WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday, Mr. STuaar RENDEL moved the second reading of the

PLACES OF WORSHIP ENFRAN-CHISRMIINT BILL.

Its object, he said, was to enable religious bodies to I acquire the freehold of their places of worship. In Wales the difficulty of obtaining the freehold was felt to be not only irksome but a stigma cast upon the relig- ion the Nonconformists professed. Dr. RENTOUL moved the rejection of the bill, objecting to its compulsory powers and holding that it would create a species of endowment of reiigious bodice. Mr. S. Everis said the bill sought powers to compulsorily acquire leasehold sites upon paying full value for the land, and to benefit the weaker as well as the stronger sects. Mr. WILLox supported the bill. Mr. STANLEY LEIGHTON opposed it, and was anxious to know whether the buildings when acquired would be used for boisteroies political meetings as well as for religious worship. Mr. ASQUITH said the [louse was simply asked by the bill to give the religious bodies the power of acquiring for themselves that which every humane and reasonable landlord would give them without the interveution of an Act of Parliament—pro- tection against confiscation and security of tenure, a privilege which the Church of England already possessed. There was not the slightest risk of the powers given by the bill being used for any other than a religious purpose. Why, he asked, had chapels been used for political purposes ? 13,•cause the schoolroom of the parish made and main- tained out of the public funds,was too often in the hands of an irresponsible body of managers who would only allow the place to be used for the purposes of one politi- cal party. The facts would have justified the introduc- tion of a more drastic measure, a measure which would have enabled religious bodies to acquire compulsorily,in all cases at a fair price,such sites as they needed to carry on their places of worship. Anyhow, the Government heartily supported the second reading.. Mr. STUART WORTLEY, Mr. PERKS, Mr. LEES KNOWLES, and Sir G. OSBORNE MORGAN continued the debate, the latter assert- ing that the bill did not go far enough. Mr. ADDISON and Mr. MACDONA supported the bill. The motion for the second reading was carried unanimously amid loud Liberal cheers, and the bill was referred to the Grand Committee on Law.

MISCELLANF,OUS.

After discussion the Cheap Trains (London) Bill was read a second time and referred to a Select Committee. The House went into Committee on the Trade Union Provident Funds Bill,which passed without amendment, was reported, and read a third time. Among the bills read a second time were the Jurors' Remuneration and Mr. l)olurmxtEa's Railway Servants' Hours Bill. Mr. T. M. HEALEY moved the second reading of the New Licenses (Ireland) Bill ; but on Mr. JAMES LOWTHER as- certaing that he referred to public-house licenses he said he must object, and the motion was postponed. The House adjourned at five minutes to six o'clock.

HE DOESN'T WANT TO BOTHER ANY-

BODY.

At Bromsgrove on Thursday Holman Meunier was committed for trial at the Worcester Assizes on the charge of murdering Mrs. Pearcey at Lickey End. He said; when asked if he wished to be defended, that he " did not want to bother anybody about it." .

===== 0002601_18930225_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
-*-- Tip COTTON STRIKE.

_NO SETTLEMENT.

A conference of members of the Executive Committee of the South-East Lancashire Employers' Federation and of the Operatives' Associations concerned in the dispute in the cotton spinning industry was held on Thursday, in Manchester. The operatives submitted the proposals agreed upon at their general meeting as terms of settle- ment. The employers stated that they were prepared to open the mills on a present reduction in wages of 24 per cent., to be followed by a further reduction of 24 per cent, in three months without any reference to the then condition of trade. They also tail down a series of propositions for the future conduct of the industry. The operatives declined to accept these terms, and re- moved their proposals with some additions. These the employers declined, and the conference ended without effecting any settlement.

SAD DEATH OF A WIGAN SOLICITOR.

A painful discovery was made at the Wigan Conserva- tive Club, on Thursday morning, soon after eight o'clock when the dead body of a young gentleman named Osmond Norman Delamere Bouth was found in the lavatory. The door being locked the attendant forced it open, and Mr. Bouth was found on the floor in a position which indicated that he had had a fit. He was subject to fits, and it was his habit when be felt them coming on to retire to a room and lock himself in. It is supposed that while in the club on Wednesday he felt an Attack coming on and retired to the place named, and expired in the fit. The deceased was the son of Dr. F. W. D. Bouth, J.P.. cotton spinner, Woodfields, Leigh, and was about 23 years of age. He was educated a Oxford, and afterwards articled at the office of Messrs Peace and Ellis, solicitors, Wigan, wnarti he was ye popular.

SIR WILFRID LAWSON AND LIQUOR

LEGLSLAIION.

PRESENTATION TO LADY LAWSON. The portrait of Sir Wilfred Lawson, the gift of the members of the United Kingdom Alliance,was presented on Thursnay to Lady Lawson by Archdeacon Farrar at a meeting of the London Auxiliary at the Mansion House. Sir Wilfred Lawson accepted the gift and the address ac- companying it as evidence of the interest now taken in the temperance movement. He was, he said, often styled the" Apostle of Temperance," but he was not a greater advocate of temperance than anybody else. Everybody was an advocate of temperance now, the licensed victualler especially. Mr. Goschen and Mr. Ritchie were apostles of temperance. They brought in bills which they thought would promote temperance,but other people thought they would not. All he had done was to advocate the control of liquor traffic. In the present Parliament they had a majority pledged to vote in their favour, ano the Government, he believed, would bring in an upright, straightforward bill. If they only introduced a halting measure it r,would be a source of misery and disaster to them.

THE CHICAGO EXHIBITION.

THE " SUNLIGHT " Exinur.

Certainly one of the most original and artistic exhibits at the forthcoming Chicago Exhibition will be that of Messrs. Lever Bros., Ltd., of Port Sunlight, near Birken- head, the manufacturers of the celebrated sunlight soap. Their exhibit consists of a magnificent model of Windsor Castle, built to scale, and covering an area of 45 feet by 18 feet, the height from the top of the central tower to the ground being about 16 feet. This beautiful model, correct in every detail, has been built from drawings prepared by Mr. Max Clarke, A.R.1.8.A., by Messrs. Campbell, Smith and Co., of "Old London" and "The Victory" fame, and is the largest of its kind in the world, and the only existing model of the castle architecturally exact. The stand is seven feet from the ground, and is divided in the centre by a draped entrance into the interior.. All round the walls are panels between pillars on w:iich hang the various works of art published by the firm. The effect of this stand is produced by an ex- quisite harmony of sunlight tints. Round the whole is a counter three feet from the ground, decorated Lanr crusts Walton. The entrance leads into a circular ves- tibule artistically decorated with frescoes, representing, by ancient shipping, the commerce of the world. The richness of the colour and design of these frescoes is superb. The vestibule opens into a spacious reception- room, also beautifully decorated with frescoes, represent- ing sunlight at various periods of the day. From the pale light of early dawn the eye passes to the blazing splendour of a noon-day sun, thence to the softer hues of later afternoon,-which finally deepen int 9 the golden glories of the closing day.

VALUE OF PROPERTY IN LIVERPOOL.

£5O A SQUARE YARD.

One of the most important transactions in real estate in Liverpool which has recently occurred is the sale by the Bank of Liverpool of the freehold premises at present occupied by the Commercial Bank, and of the adjoining groperty fronting Castle-street, Cook-street, and Bar- rington-street. The price obtained by private treaty f•r the lot is understood to be £150,000, or equivalent to nearly £5O per square yards.

DESPERATE RACIAL FIGICT IN AMERICA.

CawAcio, SATURDAY.—A desperate light occurred on Saturday, between a number of Polish 'and Hungarian workmen employed at the steel mills here. The men, after having been paid their wages began drinking heavily, and ultimately a conflict arose between the two nationalities. Knives, clubs, and sticks were the wea- pons used, and in the melee six men were injured, three of them so seriously that they are not expected to recover.

ENCOUNTER WITH A MAD DOG AT WARRINGTON.

Constable Marsh had an unpleasant duty to per- form on Tuesday night, and one which proved to be of exciting interest to over 100 persons. The duty was that of killing a half mad dog, which had placed itself at the door of Clare's riblic house, at Bridge Foot, and for a time kept the liquor traffic in that quarter suspended. No one was able to leave or enter the public house for almost an hour. Constable Marsh bad been informed of the whereabouts of the dog and repaired there. A large crowd had collected in the street, removed at a safe distance from the animal, and they watched the con- stables manceuvres. He stepped gingerly to the corner of the doorway, peeped round and got the bearings of the dog, and then struck at it with his staff. The animal sprang at him, and then retired into a corner, howling in a most distressing manner. rhe officer continued his tactics, and the dog continued to spring at him, until at length it succeeded in setting its teeth in his hand. Two or three of the crowd then rushed up and beat the ani- mal off, and when Constable Marsh had sucked the blond from the bite, he renewed his att•tck. Unfortunately, the shortness of his staff prevented him getting a proper blow at the animal, and instead of being able to "knock it on the head" at once,he had to kill the dog by beating it about the body. The constable, who received two bites, had the wounds burned and dressed immediately the dog was dead.

RUNCORN RURAL SANITARY AUTHORITY.

The fortnightly meeting was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. J. Mclntyre presided and there were present the Rev. J. W. N. Tanner, Messrs. J. Baguley, R. Aaron, J. Weir, J. stokes, J. Parr, J. M. Williams, T. Lewis, J. Brandreth, W. Turner, T. Baiter, C.C., P. Hall, W. Wilkinson, W. . _

Whitlow, J. Knowles, A. Greenway, J. Whitlow, J. W. Baguley, J. Ashton (clerk), G. Ashton (deputy clerk), Dr. Adams (medical officer), J. Farrington (nuisance inspector.)

THE WATER SUPPLY AND THE SEWERAGE OF HELSBY. The Committee appointed to consider the letter of the Local Government Board, of the 28th November, 1892, with respect to the sewerage of Helsby, reported that they held a meeting at the Railway Inn, Helsby, on the 18th inst., under the presidency of Mr. H E. Heywood. The Committee after making an inspection of the pre- sent sewerage system of the township adjourned. The following facts with regard to the sewerage and water scheme had been ascertained and were presented by Mr. Brandreth :—The acreage of the village is 1,243, the population 1,000, the rateable value for the poor £7,048 13s. 4d.,the rateable value for sanitary purposes, houses, &c. £3,358 3s.4d.,ditto and for land £3,690 10s. The rate- able assessment at id. in the £ will produce for sanitary purposes £l7 Hs. ed. There are 242 houses of which two are assessed over £BO ; nine over £4O ; 21 over £2O ; 52 over £10; 73 over £5 and 85 under £5. The district is agricultural, and the houses are scattered over the place. The sewerage at the farms are turned into mid- dens (open), and at the largest houses into cesspools, and kept there until the proper season of the year when it is carted on the land as manure. The land is so poor that crcps cannot be obtained from it without manure. The surface of the land is uneven, some points being 400 feet higher than others. The nature of the place is red sandstone, the lanes are sand, and in flood rains sometimes tons of sand are washed down to the ditches and drains. The dis- tance from the village to the river Mersey is about two miles, no houses intervening, and the land is meadow- land, nearly all in grass and separated by dykes or ditches and running streams. The water from these being taken inte the Manchester Ship Canal. An engineer Mr. George Crossland Taylor, who has resided in the village for about five years, and who has studied and knows the place well, states that the cost for carrying out the undertaking drainage,and sanitary arrangements will not be less than £7,000 besides a regular permanent charge per annum for looking after it. A description is given of the present drainage system, and it is stated that the drainage done in pipes, is done in short lengths and properly trapped and taken a safe distance to pre- vent a nuisance and turned into open ditches which the farmer empties on his land. A deputation from Helsby, consisting of Mr. G. L. Davies, J P., Mr. G. Crossland Taylor, Mr. H Lowe, and Mr. F. Thornley, waited upon the authority to present a petition with respect to the provision of a water supply, and of a sewerage scheme for Helsby. In introducing the deputation Mr. Davies said they wanted something doing as to sewerage, but they felt that they wanted water first. Most of the wells in Helsby did not give a supply of pure water, and tnere had been a great deal of typhoid fever in the township. He and his family had suffered from the disease. The petition said the petitioners considered that the occupiers of Helsby were in need of a good water supply quite as much, if not more, than in need of the sewerage scheme proposed by the Local Government Board and the petitioners humbly prayed that a committee corn. posed of the folhwing owners and occupiers, viz., Messrs. James Brandreth, Frederick Thorely, G. C. Taylor, G. L. Davies, A. J. Briant, Joseph Taylor, Joseph Eilams, Edwin J. Guest, William Guest, James White, Enoch Blythe, Frederick Whiteley, P. Huntington, A. Whalley (secretary Telegraph Company), T. Grime and H. Lowe, witteMr. John Ashton, clerk to the Guardians, and Mr. Farrington, nuisance inspector to advise them and Mr. H. Knight, shorthand writer of Helsby, as secretary, be appointed a parochial committee with full powers to advise the Sanitary Authority as to the require- ment of Helsby Parish, concerning the sewerage scheme and water supply. The petitioners were fully convinced that the water supply of Helsby from wells is in many cases thoroughly contaminated with sewerage, and are a standing danger to the public health, besides lowering the value of property, also the sewerage demands close attention. The petitioners further prayed that Mr. W. H.Radford, C.G.,Nottingham, or other equally competent engineers,to get out the preliminary schemes for the guid- ance of the Parochial Committee, to be carried out as the Authority thought fit. It was stated that the petition had been signed by 122 ratepayers representing considerably more that half the rateable value of the township.—Mr.J.Stokes : Has there been a ratepayers meeting held about this question.— Mr.'Taylor : No. There has been on the water scheme.— Mr.Brandreth : And the meeting went against the water scheme.—Mr. Lowe: By twelve, and the week after- wards they said they would have voted for the scheme. —ln reply to Mr. Stokes, the Clerk said the occupiers would have to bear the cost of a water scheine.—Mr. Stokes : Can we touch the owner.—The Chairman : No. —Mr. Stokes said a water scheme would throw open a 'large area of land for building purposes. The scheme would be for the permanent benefit of the owners and the owners went scot free.—Mr. Taylor said they wanted water and they meant to have it, and they would be prepared to do something towards sewerage.—Mr. Aaron : Then make the owners Of property do it.—Mr. Lowe: You cannot. Mr. Lowe said when he was a member of,the Board he was always against extravag- ance and he was there supporting that petition as an economist. If they got a water scheme they could do with a less sewerage scheme.—The deputation retired and the Board after consideration decided that the ques- tion should be deferred until the ratepayers had been consulted at their annual meeting and that should a parochial Committee be formed the townships of Alvan- ley and Manley should be included.—The deputation were called into the room, and informed of the Authority's decision. THE SMALLPDX EPIDEMIC. EXTRAORDINARY PROCEEDINGS. ' The Committee appointed to deal with the matters connected with the temporary hospital erected at Mosire held a meeting on the 13th inst., when it was decided that the Clerk write to the Manchester Ship Canal Co., I asking for what period they could occupy the present site. Thanks were accorded to Mr. Jackson, contractor for the kind assistance he had rendered in the provision of the hospital. It was resolved that one of Washing- ton Lyons patent steam disinfectors with Alliott and Paton' Vacuum apparatus (patented) be purchased and erected on the land at Moore. Permission waS given li.spector Farrington to travel by rail as far as practi- cable, and otherwise by conveyance, the costs to be defrayed. by the Authority. The medical officer was authorised to prepare rules for the conduct of the nurses and patients at the hospital. At a meeting ef the Com- mittee on the fifteenth inst., the Committee confirmed the resolution passed at the previous meeting and it was further decided that a conveyance be obtained for the removal of bedding and clothing for disinfection, that a man and woman be engaged to assist in the work at the hospital, that Dr. Adams be appointed to provide medi- cal attendance at the hospital at a salary of £5 ss. per week, the appointment to be determined by a months' notice; that contribution orders be made on the respective overseers for such an amount as one penny in the £ on the rateable value of the Authority's district would produce.—Mr. J. M. Williams chairman of the Committee, proposed tha adoption of the minutes.—Mr. Heywood seconded Mr. Aaron and Mr. Wilkinson pro- tested agitiajat paying five guineas a week to a superin- tendent of the hospital.—Mr. Williams and Mr. Heywood urged that the remuneration was not excessive for the duties to be performed.—Dr, Adams said as be had told the Committee, he preferred not to have the appoint- ment.—The minutes were approved without dissent.— Several members of the Committee said they would have retired from the Committee had the minutes been rejected..—Mr. Stokes moved that the Committee be strengtheeed by other members of the Authority, but Mr. Heywood moved that the Committee be constituted of the whole authority. The amendment was carried. The Medical Officer reported that since the last meet- ing, there had been three cases of smallpox at Stockton Heath, one at Walton, one at Rock Savage, and one at Halton.—Mr. Stokes mentioned a case where the patient was visited be neighbours and children, and said until there was better isolatiOn, and some means of prevent- ing such intercourse, they need net be surprised at the spread of infectious diseases.—Dr. Adams also gave an example of how the disease was spread.— -Mr. Weir said at Stockton Heath two women visited a patient and then entered a Warrington 'bus. The driver on; being

told where they had been, turned them out, and the,' then attempted tfs enter another 'bus.—Dr. Adams said there was no law to prevent that.—Several Members The sooner there is and the better.—Mr. Parr said this smallpox was going to be a very costly affair, and it was not a very good time to put extra expense on the ratepayers, but he thought they were like Bealain's ass and bore the burden very patiently. He thought if Lard Egerton were properly approached by a nice letter from the Authority, asking him to contribute towards the ex- pense he would do so. He was a large land owner ia the district, a good landlord,Chairman of the Ship Canal Co., and when he was member of Parliament for that division for some years, las had the support of the far' mere and the landed interest and past favours, he cOO- - called for some gratitude. He proposed that such a letter be sent.—Mr. Wilkinson seconded tee motion.—Mr.Stokes said he had intended to have owe a resolution on similar lines with respect to the Shill Canal.—Mr. Parr: If it had not been for the Ship Canal we should not have had the disease.—The Chairman moved an amendment that Mr. Parr be deputed to Moe the facts before his lerdship. (Laughter.)—Mr. seconded.—Mr. Parr : I don't agree with that at all. What is the good of one single individual, it is preposter- ous. (Laughter.) The subject was referred to the Cuu''' mittee. Mr. Leader Williams wrote-with respect to the Sinali- pox Hospital at Moore, that the Ship Canal Co. could not consent to the building remaining permanently 00 their land, but there would be no difficulty in giving tile Authority the use of the hospital until the end of ttic year. The Local Government Board, in acknowledeing letter from the Authority with reference to the provisi‘!a of a temporary infectious diseases hospital for them district, stated that where it was not intended to by means of a loan the expense of providing hospital accommodation for cases of infectious disease, the Board's sanction is not required, as the Author possessed the requisite site power under section 131 01 the Public Health Act, 1875, including the power of Pro. vidin,g, the requisite nursing and medical attendants. At the same time,the Board stated they were not iri tha habit of entertaining applications as to loans for teo- porary hospital buildings except in certain cases whelal these buildings may be in extension of existing perrna7' ent • accommodation on approved sites.—The Board g experience of temporary hospitals is that they are rarelY adapted to their intended purposes and that they ha,V. often acted as det.rren's to the provision of accommous. tlon suited to the requirements of the district. At a later meeting of the Authority Mr. Pair acal" brought forward his motion, Mr. Stokes seconding. After some discussion the motion was allowed to drop.
