THE DEMANDS OF MILITARISM.

A meeting under the auspices of the Wycliffe Young People's Society, Warrington, was held on Monday evening, when a deputation ,froir the Liverpool Branch of the Peace Society spoke on the above named subject. There was a good atten- dance. The Rev. John Tong° briefly introduced Mr. Lewis to the meeting. Mr. Lewis said by militarism he net only meant preparation for war, but war itselt. He not only meant those demands which were taking place, but also those demands which had been met in times gone by, and he also included the demands upon tne nation for the soldier. The devotees of this god of war claimed that to prevent war it was necessary to prepare ourselves for war, an,d also that the training of the soldier was good for his physique. The English navy had between 500 or 600 floating machines, and yet they still wished to increase it in order to make it larger than two other nations combined. There were about 300 admirals in this country, or enough to supply the whole world. The Speaker referred to the boys brigade, and to the action of the late Postmaster General in introducing military drill into the telegraph service. Mr. Lewis also gave statistics showing the great de- mends %bat bad been made for human life. He con- tended that militarism stepped in between man and God, as it demanded that man should obey his superior officer, even if against his own conscience.—Mr. James Crossfield moved the following resolution :—" That this meeting petitions Parliament to take the necessary steps to induce H.M.'s Government to open up communica- tions with the President of the United States with a view to the conclusion of a treaty of arbitration between this country and America, for the settlement of any international disputes that may arise."—Mr. Crosfield in support of the resolution instanced the amicable feel- ing existing between Great Britain and America, since the settlement of the Alabama claims. Although the sum paid by Great Britain was considered by many people exorbitant, he calculated that if war had taken place between the two nations' more money would have been spent in one month with a less satis- factory result. When nations went to war it did not always follow that the conquerors were in the right. Referring to our policy in Egypt and Uganda he ridi- culed many of the reasons given in support of con- tinuing our occupations of those countries. Re said it was quite surprising to see the great interest that bad arisen with regard to missionary enterprise, by those who had hitherto taken little or no interest in it. He hoped we were nct going to have a second Egypt in Uganda. He would like to hear of a branch of the Peace Society being formed in Warrington.—Mr. Farrar seconded the resolution, which was supported by Mr. Birch and Mr. rickavance, and carried with only one dissentient. A vote of thanks to Mr. Lewis, Mr. Cross- field, and the Rev. J. Yonge was passed on the propo- sition of Councillor Greening, seconded by Mr. R.Wylie. A petition to Parliament bearing the resolution as ' largely signed at the close of the meeting.

SENSATIONAL EPISODE IN A BANK.

A sensation was caused at Gurney's Bank, Norwich, on Saturday, by a farmer named William Wright, of Norfolk, cutting his throat with a clasp knife, while his nephew, who accompanied him, was counting slips in a banking transaction. Wright died about twe hours afterwards, at the Norfolk and Norwich Illspital, to which place he had been conveyed. The affair took place during the busiest hours at the bank, about torty or fifty customers being present at the time. Family bereavements are said to account for the mental aberra- tion of the deceased.

===== 0002601_18930225_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. DE COBAIN ON HIS DEFENCE.

Mr. de Cobain, late member for East Belfast, who was remanded on Saturday on certain criminal charges, was released at a late hour on Sunday night, the necessary bail having been lodged with the police. Mr. de Cobain, who remained in gaol from Saturday afternoon until the production of the bail, went after his release to his resi- dence at Hampton House, which he owns, together with other property in Belfast. The local detective depart- ment are busily engaged completing the evidence for the resumption of the case to-day (Saturday). Mr. de Cobain, who vehemently protests his innocence, has been in Belfast since the end of January,

THE EIGHT HOURS' DILL

DEPUTATION OF MINERS TO MR. GLADSTONE. The Prime Minister has consented to receive a deputa- tion from the National Miners' Federation on the after- noon of March 3, in favour of an eight hours' working day. The right hon. gentleman will on the same occa- sion receive a deputation from the miners of Aberdare and.Merthyr, who desire to urge reasons against the pro- posed legal limitation of the hours of labour. Mr. D. A. Thomas, one of the members for Merthyr, intends to move the rejection of the Miners' Eight Hours Bill when it comes up for second reading in the House of Commons.

===== 0002601_18930225_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PAYMENT OF MEMBERS. MR. LABOUCII2IRE'S BILL.

- - -- Mr. Labouchere, in his bill to authorise the payment of members for their Parliamentary services, intends to propose that the payment to each member should be 15s. per diem all the year round, with an addition of a season ticket between London and the locale of the con- stituency he represents. The clause as to how the money is to be paid has to be left out, as a member can- not make a charge upon the Treasury ; but if the Bill reach the Committee stage, it can be recommitted in order that the point may be dealt with. The names OR the back of the bill, in addition to Mr. Labouchere's, are those of Sir Charles Dilke, Dr. Hunter, Mr. 11. T. Reid, Mr. Jacoby, Mr. Kearley, Dr. Clark, Mr. Picton, Mr. S. T. Evans, and Mr. Storey.

ANTI-HUM BUG.

BUDDHISII AND CIIRISTIANITY.

Sir,—ln resuming my notice of the above subject. it is necessary to clear the ground of some confusion in which it at present lies. Your correspondent " Harry Esmond" began by referring to the "remarkable points of similarity between Buddhism and Christianity," and forthwith set off to draw a parallel between the Brah- man-god Chrishna and Christ. Apparently your cor- respondent was not aware that Buddhism and Brahman- ism are two very different religions. The former was an absolute revolt from the latter in the person of Sakyit- Muni, or Gautama in the sth century before Christ. This great reformer renounced the whole system of the Brahmanic pantheon with its priests, its sacrifices, its ritual and castes, proclaiming the universal brotherhood of humanity, and the great doctrine of self-renunciation. Be carried his teachings however to the extreme of denying the existence of a supreme being: or of the human soul and of immortality—offering only as the goal of happiness to his disciples the state of Nirvana— or eternal sleep. Fearful opposition to his teachings was aroused—but the marvellous influence of his, in many respects, beautiful and beneficient life rose superior to it all ; and though the conflict was long and severe, Buddha at length so far triumphed that the -ad orthodox Brahmans accepted him as the Oth Incarnation of Vishnu. For ages he wielded his immense influence over the mind of the Hindus, until between the Bth and 9th century of our Era a tierce persecution, and opposi- tion to his doctrines on the part of the Brahmans arose, and became so violent that Buddhism was all but ex- pelled from India, and at this day has no place there ex- cept in the small district of Nepal. It is therefore a strange entanglement of the subject to announce" points of resemblance between Buddhism and Christianity " and then to illustriate by parallels between Christ and the Breslanzan-god Chrishna. Yet this is what " Harry Esmond "does, enumerating particulars of resemblance in the fables of Chrishna which he says " agree almost word for word with the account given by St. Matthew of Christ." Now what I want especially to make clear in this letter is, that it is utterly useless to discuss any point of resemblance of fact or incident in the histszy of either Buddha or Christ, or of the same between Chrishna and Christ ; since with the exception of a trifling incid- ent or two, there is no evidence whatever of the exist- ence of such resemblances in any Hindoo literature earlier than the beginning of our own era. This applies equally to Buddha and to Chrishna. In fact the " remarkable points of similarity" are traceable to Christian influence alone, which reaching India early in the first and second century supplied the Brahmans with all the sources necessary for their manipulation of legends concerning Buddha, and their god Chrishna. I make this statement on the authority of eminent Sanskrit scholars, such as Sir Monier Williams, Dr. Rhys Davids, and the translator of the Bhagavah-Gita, the rev. John Davies, who particularly handles this questio, in an appendix to his version of this famous poem. Nor must I omit specially to mention, on the testimony of Professor Williams, that those very legends of Chrishna the most like to the ineidents in the life of Christ appear in Hindoo literature, which dates back no further than the sth or 6th century after Christ. In conclusion let me say that the facts related by Max Muller,and quoted in my previous letter, would on any ground justify our suspicion of Brahmanic frauds]; but when the period of the literature to which we are indebted for our knowledge of these "remarkable points of resemblance" is taken into account, one can only dismiss the whole matter as something worse than idle talk. But whilst expressing myself thus strongly on tbis particular feature of the subject I am most heartily ready to admit that there are truly won- derful points of comparison between the moral teach, ings and conduct of Buddha, and the character aad teachings of Christ. But I must tell your correspondent that when Christians have ventured to stigmatise Buddhists as " heathens and barbarians "it has not been on the ground of Buddha's lofty ideal and moral character. Yru have only to go to lands where Budd- hism prevails, and the contrast between Buddha's influ- ence and that of Christ will more than justify the epithets of "heathen and barbarian." But this phase of the subject—the moral teachings of Buddhism, both in resemblance and contrast, to the religion of. Christ, as also the "little success of Christian missionaries among the teeming millions of India and China " I hope to deal with in future letters,—Yours obediently. JOHN DIGNUM. Penketh, February 23rd, 1893.

BUDDHISM AND CHRISTIANITY. SOME POINTS Or RESEMBLANCE.

Sir,—Mr. Dignum's attention has been called to several letters which have appeared in your columns on the above subje,t, and as he is an assumed authority on all matters of a scholastic character, he, ef course, rushes in to print to set the writers of these letters straight. This is exactly what I wanted someone to do, and so I welcome his letter as helping to disseminate a knowledge of this very interesting subject. It is a pity, however, that he could not refrain from coarse and ungentle- manly insinuations against myself and "Eclectic.' It had not been suggested to my mind that the people of Warrington were exceedingly gullible, nor had I the least intention of gulling them. The statements I made can easily be verified by anyone who will take the trouble to read only a little of the very voluminous iterature on the subject. I can only smile at Mr. Dignum's advice that I should study Max Muller's " Science of Religion.- I can promise him that he shall have considerably more of Max Muller than will be at all to his liking before this correspondence shall have finished. Indeed, lam amazed that he should so much as mention Max Muller in this connexion, and can only suspect that his acquaintance with this savant's writings is of a very superficial character. But, after all, who thinks of advancing Max Muller as an autko- rity to-day ? The various articles in his books were given as lectures about thirty years ago, and the " Science of Religion" has made considerable progress since then. But we will deal with Max Muller further on. Mr. Dignum objects to my " setting' of the points of resemblance between Buddhism and Christianity, and calls attention to what he terms my glaring inaccuracy touching Chrishna, whom I called a Buddist god---" but who," says Mr. Dignum, "was nothing of the kind." I accept the correction. To be correct Chrishna was a Brahman god. Max Muller calls him the" lovely shep- herd god." Slme writers speak of him as the Hindu saviour. Brahmanism is the oldest form of Hindu religion. Buddhism is founded on Brahmanism, but is a sort of revolt or protest against it, just as Protestant- ism is a protest against Roman Catholicism. (See Clodd's " Childhood of Religions.") There are various sects amongst the Hindus even as there are amongst Chris- tians. Now, anyone reading Mr. Dignum's letter would con- clude—unless they had a knowledge of the subject—that I had attempted to impose a nnmber of idle and mis- leading tales upon my readers—that, in fact, I had stated what was not true. Well, I intend to substantiate my statements, by quoting, in the first place, several pas- sages from the " Encyclopedia Brittannica." (I hope Mr. Dignum will admit that this work is somewhat of an authority.) These passages deal with Buddhism only and as Chrishna was a Bratinsan god I will deal with him in another letter. In my next letter I will give passages from Max Muller's books" The Science of Reli- • gion, and "Chips from a German workshop." "We are accustomed," says the Encyclopedia Brit- . tannica, "to find the legendary and the miraculous, ' gathering like a halo, around the early history of refi- i gious leaders,until the sober truth runs the risk of being altogether neglected for the glittering and edifying false- hood. Buddha has not escaped the fate which has be- alien the founders of other religions, and as late as the

year 1854 the late Professor Wilson, of Oxford, read a paper before the Royal Asiatic Society of London, in which he mairealned that the supposed life of Buddha was a myth, and Buddha himself merely an imaginary being. No one, however, would now support this view, and it is admitted that under the mass of miraculous tales which have been handed down regarding him,there is a basis of truth already sufficiently clear to render possible an intelligible history,which will become clearer and clearer as older and better authorities are made ac- cessible." A list of Byddhist books is then given, of which it says, "These have their miraculous incidents, too—the relation of the Sanskrit sources to the Pali re- sembling in many respects that of the apocryphal gos- pels to the New Testament." An account is then given of the life of Buddha. He was born ot royal parentage ; became a penniless and despised student and a homeless wanderer. Mara, the tempter, appsared in the sky,and Buddha was tempted like Christ. He was offered a uni- versal dominion over the four great continents ; but, like Christ, he resisted the tempter. He was called the "Enlightened One"—a teacher and saviour. Buddha teaches his followers to attain freedom from impurity and revenge and evil longings of all kinds ; to purify their minds from all unholy desires and passions. Again, the first enemy which the awakened believer has to tight against is sensuality, and the last is unkindliness ; it is impossible to build anything on a foundation of mire, and the topstone of all that one can build, the highest point he can reach,the point above purity, above justice. above even faith, is, according to Buddha, universal charity. Till he has gained that the believer is still bound, he is not free, his mind is still dark ; true en- lightenment, true freedom are complete only in love." " Buddha is recorded to have performed many miracles, and in his youth to have surpassed all his contempora- ries in feats of bodily and mental skill, and even to have taught his teachers-legends bearing a curious resemblance to th* apocryphal gospel of the infancy of Jesus." Ac- cording to the "Encyclopedia Brittannica " Buddha died 400 years before Christ. Max Muller assigns ths same dates as that of his death. The " Enc. Britt." then goes on to speak of the "shaven priests of Buddhism," its bells and rosaries, its images and holy water, its popes. and bishops, its abbots and monks of many grades, its processions and feast days, its confessional etnd purga- tory, and its worship of the double Virgin, so strongly I resembling Romanism that the first Catholic missiona- ries thought it must he an imitation by the devil of the religion of Christ." This account is a direct confirma- tion of Clodd, whom I quoted in my first letter. On Sunday afternoon, February sth, a dignitary of the Church of England, Archdeacon Wilson. gave an addrosss at the Parish Church, Rochdale. on " The an- cient religion of India." In the course of his address he said, " The morality inculcated in their sacred books" (the Hindus) " stood on a very lofty level, and even in practice there were points in which their morality was universally beyond that of any Christian nation. The degradation of drunkenness was regarded by them as in the first degree of crime. With us it is condoned." HARRY ESMOND.

afternoon wore on the storm appeared to, be getting 1 worse, whilst at dinner a decided falling off of hungry souls was noticeable. Our little party seemed happy and gay, and thoroughly enjoying our first dinner, indeed, I at our table of ten there were but two yaJant chairs. At table : Mr. Curdy : Well, Mr.Thomas,what will:you take? Mr. Thomas : Port wine. Mr. Curdy : How's that sir, usually you take whisky ? Mr Thomas : True, but I prefer port in a storm. After draining his glass he was seen to rest his head on his hand, an said he would take a rest.—Exit Mr. Thomas.

Once more leaving the saloon a breath of fresh air is very refreshing. "What a splendid idea," everyone cries, as it is noticed the promenade deck is lit up with incandescent lights, turning night into day, and making it possible to sit down and read, enjoying the fresh sea breezes in place of betaking one's-self to the old- fashioned close cabin. Then, again, no risk is run of striking against any obstruction on the deck, thereby rendering a walk in the evening enjoyable. Whether from the abundant supply of fresh ozone, or from the fact of being tired, we seek our cabin early, and there meet our room mate already ensconced in his berth. He was an English gentleman resident in New York, and crossing to visit his home and friends, and proved to be a very pleasant companion. (To be continued.)

NOMS ON GAMES AD

AfHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

Our home team enjo-y-ed—a-;r-i-c-t-o-ry and some mudlark- ing at Kendal, when they met the Hornets of that locality on Saturday. They played an excellent game, just as they always seem to do now, when they go out ot town. It is probably a change of air that has the beneficial effect. Ido know for certain that a change of hair would have a beneficial effect upon a certain player's looks, after a match. But " that's neither hare nor thare," as the young collegiate said.

About 1,000 spectators welcomed the two teams as they waded—l use the term advisedly—on to the field. The slowly dropping, dribbling, dripper of the previous a hours' rain had made that field a regular swamp. And after the game had been going a few minutes one could detect many of the players clasping chunks of mud as affectionately as an average child. This liquid condition of the ground was, of course, detrimental to the play, hut strange to say, the Hornets couldn't play at all ou their own mud, whilst the previous experience of War- rington enabled them to score a try, and some other points which the referee would not allow them. This brings me to a point of complaint. Both P.R. Lawless and John Bate scored points which the referee, without any explanation, disqualified.

Warrington's play was far and away above that of the home men, and although they nominally only won by a try, the victory was really beyond tile range of four points. The forwards played a" clinking" game; they combined better and showed an all-round general improvement. Boscow, by the way, came out in a brilliant fashion. His general agility, his smart tactics, clever saving, quick punting and good fielding made even the Zendalites cheer lustily. Lawless played a good game again, and was up to the scratch with his tackling.

I was glad to hear of Frank Turner playing again ; he ought to get a right royal welcome when he appears before the Warrington public.

Warrington A team completely routed the Widnes A team on Saturday. They came out victors to the tune of 4-1-5 to nil. Barnes, Massey, Ash urst, and Lally were very much in evidence, and Hampson played a game which points to gradual improvement and better finish.

Broughton, so to speak, came out of their shell in the competition match with Salford. Had it not been for the poor play of two of the back rank, it is generally conceded that they would have done the trick with Sal. ford. The results of the competition stand thus at pre- sent ;

ASSOCIATION.

Local lovers of the Association game will have an opportunity to-day of seeing about tne best Association match that will have marked the season in Warrington. The St. Elphins club and W .2k.C.. are going to try con- clusions providing the weather permits.

The following are the selected teams :—St. Elphins— Goal, H. Bates ; right back, D. Marshall ; left back, Rev. T. W. Cook; half backs, R. Jolley, J. Pheonia, J. Clayton; right wing, J. Colley, Hickenbottom ; left wing, .1. Hutton, C. Taylor; centre, F. Scarisbrick (cap- tain) ; reserves, J. Gibson, and W. Whittle ; umpire. G. Squire. Warrington Association.—Goal, J. The Warrington Amateur Athletic Club, which has only been in existence a few months, can now boast of having nearly 150 members. Judging by the attendance of the members on club nights it bids fair for success. I bear that it is intended to hold a grand assault at arms about the end of March and the Committee have secured the services of Sergeant Major Haight, instructor to the Manchester Gymnastic Club, and a few of his pupils. An attempt is also being made to get the assistance of two of the best middle weight boxers of the North of England. The programme will be made up of boxing, lance exercise, sword v. bayonet, &c. As a tournament of this kind has not been held in Warrington before, it is likely to be a great " draw."

THE FRAUD ON ACTRESSES AT WARRINGTON. Mr. Taylor, the police court missionary Warring- ton, has received the following letter from one of the actresses in Leslie's " Up to Date" company, who were desarted by their manager.—" 13 Regent Square, Kings Cross.—Dear Sir, lam pleased to be able to inform you that I arrived safely home last evening. Thank you very much for your great kindness to me at Warrington. I shall be glad if you will convey my best thanks to the magistrates, who so kindly and promptly relieved us from the dreadful dilemma in which we were placed,through Mr. Leslie's conduct. I shall ever remember with gratitude their great kinuness,—l am, dear sir, yours truly, Alva Darrell." Letters have also been received from the other actresses.

TO ENGLAND IND BACK.

VISITING TIIE OLD COUNTRY. (No. 2.) The writer of the following account of a journey "To England and Back in the Winter eason," reides in Seattle, Washington, U.S.A., but is the Fon of a gentleman much respected in Gateacre, and the son-in-law of a well-known Warringtontan.

The American system of checking baggage is so perfect that after receiving our checks at Suspen- sion Bridge, we did not again see our baggage until placed on board.—llow very different to the principle adopted by the English railway com- panies ! In England it is el, eryone for himself and his own—and who is to prevent an unscrupulous party from taking a box or package at any station. by merely pointing it out to the guard or porter ? No doubt many thefts do occur from this cause. The Eng- lish have got a great deal to learn from their American cousins in this line.

Our baggage is safe and we are on deck. Notwith- standing the early hour arranged for the steamers de- parture, quite a number of people were assembled on the quay to bid their last adieu to friends, acquain- tances, or even all the passengers, for who could stand by and see a steamer freighted with so many human beings, even though none be known personally, without wishiag one and all a safe and speedy voyage. There were very few who were not startled by the first loud blast of the whistle, and as gangway after gangway was lowered, and first one rope and then another cast off, our ocean home was free. A long whistle sounded,iminediately followed by the dull thud of the propellers and our voyage had commenced. Backing slowly clear of the quay our last salutes were exchanged with those left behind—the waving of handkerchiefs became fainter. The steamers bow was turned to sea and at 7.55 a.m, the propellors com- menced to beat the water and drive us forward towards the East. Leaving the great city behind, and slowly passing by the Statue of Liberty, Governors island, entering the narrows to the fairway buoy at 9.54 and Sandy Hook light ship at 10.3 cur tarewell to America was ended. Many incoming and outgoing steamers were passed, one of the latter a North German Lloyd's running abreast of us for about two hours, but gradu- ally dropping behind until finally lost to view.

And now how shall I commence an account of our doings for the following week. Perhaps it would be here in place to give a few particulars of the magnificent steamer' Teutonic," that is bearing us onward to" the tight little island" nearly 3,000 miles away. Viewed from a distance, she is unlike the majority of vessels, having but three pule masts these having no yards-- they carry only steadying sails, thereby dispensing with a great deal of heavy top gear and rigging. The 'familiar black and buff funnels are of enormous size, and placed between the fore and main mast gives the vessel a trim appearance, and add to the graceful outline of the hull. Now as to the di- mensions: Her gross tonnage is 10,000 tons; her length 582 feet, her breadth 574 feet, her depth between 39 and 40 feet. Can you realise what such measure- ments mean—the enormous size of such a craft—she being the longest vessel afloat. She is built of Silmen's- Martin steel. What then must be the power of engines to drive such a vessel. Being a twin screw steamer she is fitted with 2 sets of triple -expansion engines, develop- ing nearly 18,000 horse power. In case of a broken shaft or disablqment of either set of engines, the speed would be but slightly diminished as additional power I could be given to the remaining set, doing away with the necessity of completing a trip under sail, etc., as in cases of most single propellors and engines. In case of collision the ship and passengers are well provided for. there being water-tight bulkheads running the whole length, with many sub-divisions adding greatly to the strength of the ship and making her almost unsink- able.

Hark ! is not that the gong sounding, and indeed a welcome one for early rising and fresh ozone has given us quite an appetite ; therefore it is not surprising that we should hurry into the saloon for our first meal. The allotment of seats not having taken place we find room at a side table and enjoy the tasty dishes placed before us. Ere that meal is over we have a fair idea of the number of passengers aboard. Here I might mention, that unlike most companies the" White Star" limit the number of cabin passengers to the seating accommoda- tion of their steamers' saloons, and not as on many steamers where there are two sittings, which always causes trouble, annoyance and general discomfort. Seats are allotted by the purser shortly after sail- ing and whether at the first or second sitting you are expected to be on time and to keep the same chair for the voyage—here's the annoyance , and inconvenience of such a plan, indisposition to eat at (first gong) from more causes than one—disinclination to leave a pleasant game, reading, the penning of a letter, or general conversation maybe with No. 2 table section—whereas with but one sitting so long as meals are about, you go to table at your leisure, where you will find your steward ready to do his utmost to please and satisfy you. I might say that notice was given that prior to lunch, the allotment of seats would take place—near that hour the purser appeared in the saloon and very soon had a gootily throng around him. The captain's table, generally in the centre of the saloon, is the one where the seats are most coveted ; the purser and doctor each being placed at the heads of other tables; then there are those at the side where small parties generally arrange to sit. We preferred one of the latter and were soon accommodate d.

Might I here relate a little incident bearing upon this subject that occurred to a friend and myself on our first trip across the Atlantic in the Cunard steamer" Urn- brie?" Not being then conversant with the customs on board the ocean steamers, upon entering the saloon we looked around for two vacant chairs. One of the stewards came to our relief and asked for our numbers. Thinking he referred to our state cabin berths we re- plied 11 and 12. The seats bearing these numbers hap- pened to be vacant at the time ; we occupied them, dinner .commenced, first course over, just received the second, when a party of four,two ladies and two gentle- men, came up to the table at which we were seated; general consternation, stewards in a state of excitement likewise though in a far greater degree the ladies of the party, but not least by a long way our poor selves,inno- cents that we were. We at once offered to move and made profound apologies—ignorance—first voyage— vain regrets /cc. all to no purpose. Elderly lady was decidedly vexed, indeed shocked at our action in taking their seats. However a full explanation given to the gentlemen after dinner, ended with a good hearty laugh; they enjoyed the joke immensely, and proved to be the best of friends throughout this trip. Yet somehow or other whenever the elderly lady came by our way, we felt like crawling under the table. Her first look or indignation remained impressed on my mind for a long long time.

Suppose we take it for granted our first meal is over and the tables cleared, a fine opportunity presents itself to view the handsome saloon situated on the main deck, and occupying the whole width of the vessel, offers seat- ing accommodation for 300 cabin passengers. It is most tastefully decorated, the prevailing colour being ivory and gold, the 'walls being relieved by panels on which are carved figures of tritons, nymphs and other marine symbols—the background consisting of bright gold. Heavy brass work surrounds the usually plain framed portholes, over which is an entirely new feature for night decoration—coloured glass panels (bearing tha arms of the principal cities of America and Europe) can be drawn down, thus covering the usual open spacei; behind the sliding panels an incandescent lamp illumines the glass, adding greatly to the effect. The ceiling is beautifully lined in gold—the centre being surmounted by a splendid coloured glass dome. Many small fairy like incandescent electric lights through subdued rays over this spacious and luxurious apartment at night time. Comfortable lounges, chairs, etc., give an idea of comfort to the traveller.

Passing out and ascending the staircase, we are once more on the spacious deck—some 245 feet in length, with a clear space of 18 feet on each side of the deck houses. The greater portion of this space is covered,thus making a splendid promenade for passengers in all weathers. As we mingle with our fellow passengers it is surprising to find how free and easy everyone appears, more especially those who are not making their maiden trip—and their name is apparently legion. We very soon learn that many are returning to spend Christmas in England.

Overhead the sky is beautifully clear, the air crisp and bracing, the water inclined to be a little choppy, whilst in the far distance small black clouds are to be seen collecting Shortly afterwards little white caps are on the crest of every wave—it is rumoured a storm is brew- ing. We had not to wait long for within four hours of leaving port a strong wind followed us, gradually increasing to a north-west gale. Fortunately for ml,ny of the passengers the gale aid not come too suddenly upon us, giving time to become accustomed to the alter- nate pitching and rolling of our mighty steamer. It had, however, the effect of diminishing the attendance at table for lunch, giving one the idea, that some poor mortals had no disposition to take a meal so soon after breakfast, or may be they were suffering from Mal de MCP.

Oh ! sea sickness must be a sad complaint, for my part I am thankful to say, I never suffered from the effects and never yet lost a meal when at sea. When crossing in the " Urn-, !aria" I heard a gentleman from Manchester, England, say, when advised to leave his deck chair—the rolling of the steamer making it unsafe for him to remain longer on deck, he being in a very weak state from this dreaded complaint, "I would not move or try to save myself if the ship was going down, indeed, death would be acceptable." Poor fellow; he never seemed to get over it, and was ill the whole way across. Upon arriv- ing in New York he had to be carried off the steamer on account of weakness and inability to walk. As the

MR. BRUNNER, M.P., AT NORTEINVICH.

INTERESTING SPEECH ON POLITICAL TOPICS. The annual soiree In connection with the Glad- stone Club, North wich, was held on Saturday even- ing, under the presidency of Mr. 11. Bratt, C.C. The guest of the evening was Nil% J. T. Brunner, M.P., who was accompanied by Mrs. Brunner, Miss Maud Brunner, and Mr. J. F. L. Brunner. The pro- ceedings were of an enthusiastic description. Mr. J. W. Deakin moved the following resolution : " That this meeting of Northwich Liberals cordially welcomes Mr. J. T. Brunner, the member for the divi- sion, congratulates hira on his great victory at the last election, and assures him that his loyal support of Mr. Gladstone in this sitpthme effort of his life,to bring con- tentment and prosperity to Ireland will receive the en- thusiastic approval of all true Liberals in this constitu- ency." (Applause.) The motion was seconded by Mr. .1. T. Yearsly, and carried unanimously amid cheers. Mr. J. T.BRUNNER, M.P ,who was loudly cheered, said it had been a great pleasure to him to hear the two speeches which had b en delivered, and a great pleasure to know that one of the gentlemen had made up his mind to secure a majority of 2,000, while Mr. Deakin had decided that it should be 2.500. (Applause.) A. WORD ABOUT MR. WHITELEY. fle was not at all sure that it would not be 12,000— (laughter)—for he thought if it depended upon Mr. Whiteley it would certainly reach that figure. (Laughter, and a Voice : " A walk over.") He had been very much amused to hear that that gentleman, after vowing and declaring over and over again that he would come to fight the Tory battle in the Northwich Division when- ever he was asked to, had thought he was a little safer in a place where last time the majority was in favour of a Tory, and he had betaken himself to Stockport. (Laughter.) He noticed that his first speech in Stockport was exactly the same as that made to the people of this neighbourhood. (Laughter.) He told them that the Stockport people wre highly intelligent, exactly the same as he said at Northwich. (Laughter.) 'f he con- gratulations of the Northwich Liberals had been ten- dered to him (the speaker), by kind friends in the neigh- bourhood for a considerable number of months past. THE LATE CONTEST. One passage in Mr. Yearsley's speech struck him as having a little doubtful ring. He said that at the last election they had all the elements of success in their favour. But the forces arrayed against the Liberal party in that and every other division of Great Britain were simply tremendous. (Elear, hear.) They had al- most all the wealthy people against them ; they had .one of the greatest trades in the country arrayed against them. The Tory party then had in their hands an al- most fabulous sum of money wherewith to debauch the constituencies. Perhaps they did not know as well in that,as they did in some other divisions,what transpired, for in Northwich they stole a march upon their oppo- nents, and were a little too many for them. His friend, Mr. King, had been relating to him again the joy with which he saw

THE BATTERED AND TATTERED REGIMENT that went back to the brewefies in Manchester the day after the:election. Such a sodden and sorry-looking lot of politicians it had never been his lot to see before. (Laughter.) When they considered that they might rejoice all the more that their victory was what it was. He confessed that he had no hope before the election of an increased majority, and it was only through the exertions of the gallant men he saw before him and of many of the ladies that it came about. The election was not won because they had weak forces against them, but because the work was so thoroughly well done, and becauee the hearts of all the good mon and women in that constituency were set upon a noble leader. (Applause.) He thanked them for enabling him to go , back to the House of Commons in August last when they defeated a Tory Government and put a Liberal one in its place. Mention had been made of Mr. Henry , Fowler's wise and courageous act in reducing the quali- fication for Boards of Guardians from—in this neigh- , bourhood—a £25 to £5 annual ratine. (Applause.) Now, with the reduction from £25 to £5, there would be at the hands of the ratepayers a full! free, and a wide choice, so that they would not be obliged to send to the Board of Guardians a man who was comfortable in his means' who had never felt the pinch of poverty, but they could send one who sympathised with the poor bcause he knew what it was to be poor, and he (Mr. Brunner) was certain that the result would be a happy one for them all. (Applause.) FREE EDUCATION. He ought to say a word in praise of the work that had been done by his friend Mr. Acland at the Board of Education. Mr. Acland had made up his mind that there should be no tricks played with the law, with re- Surd to free education, and he (the speaker) asked it of them, for their own sakes and for the sakes of their neighbours and children, to back Mr. Acland in his determination to see that everybody who required free education should have it at the asking. (Applause. The opportunity would be given before long, he hoped,/ of putting the matter before them in a practical shape, and he trusted that whether their children had passed their schooling days or not,they would join in the attempt to obtain free education for all out of their kindness to these who were less well off than themselves. A LONDON QUESTION SETTI ED. Beside Mr. Fowler and Mr. Acland they had had Mr. Asquith, the Home Secretary, who had settled in London a question which had troubled the people of that city for a good many years. Mr. Asquith had decided that Trafalgar-square should be open for public meet- ings under commonsense, sensible, and practical ar- rangements, while for many years the Tory Home Secretary had been resisting the efforts of the people to meet in the Square to discuss publ,c questions. It was found, as had always been the case when they allowed English people their free liberty to do what they thought right, that they were just as considerate of the interests and feelings of their neighbours as any set of people , could wish them to be. (Applause.) Last Wednesday a measure was brought in by Mr. J. W. Logan, a mem- ber from tke Midlands,providing that in all local affairs, in all local elections—or he should say very nearly all local elections, for they told him in Runcorn that morn- ing that there was an omission in the Bill which he pro- posed to supply when it got into committee, Runcorn be- ing managed by a Board of Commissioners, for which there was no provision in the Bill—people would be en- titled to vote by ballot. The Bill was called THE PARISH AND DISTRICT COUNEILS BILL. He did not know whether anyone at that meeting had seen the way in which voting papers were sometimes filled up for the elections of Local Boards. (Laughter.) He had seen something in it, and he would be very glad indeed when voting papers were done away with, and when every man should be invited to ge to the polling place to give his vote and be protected by the secrecy of the ballot. (Applause.) Naturally, as the measure was coming from a Radical Government, it would provide that each man should have one vote and no man should have more. (Hear, hear.) In addition to that, they, as a matter of course, abolished the property qualification not only for Local Boards of Health, for Burial Boards, for Boards of Commissioners, for everything, so that for the first time in the history of the ceuntry every man, rich or poor, would be equal before the law. He thanked them for giving him the opportunity when it came of voting in favour of the second reading of THE HOME RULE BILL. (Loud Applause.) They were aware that none of them had had an opportunity of recording a vete in favour of the first reading of the Bill, because the Tories had adopted the plan of the warrior of old, who made up his mind to run away in order that he might live to fight another day. (Laughter.) They, did not challenge the vote, but allowed the Bill to be brought in without any division. It was perfectly true that the contrEst between their behaviour in the House, and their attempts reiterated and repeated and pressed for years past for the details of the Bill had been most ludicrous. They were burning, they were dying to know what was in the Bill, and yet, for a whole fortnight, they had not been allowed to bring it in, because the Tories would insist in saying, over and over again that they wanted to know what was in it. At last they had the Bill before them, and it was that day textually in the newspapersd and if any man or woman with an unbiassed mind couls read the Bill and read it into any of the terrible fear, which the Tories had been expressing for seven years he should be greatly astonished. To his mind, the Bill was AN ENORMOUS IMPROVEMENT upon the Bill of 1886. It provided, in an ample and, to him, perfectly satisfactory manner, for the representa- tion of the minority of Ireland. (Hear, hear.) There was missing from it that clause to which he took very great objection in 1888 which provided for the exclusion of the Irish members from the House of Commons in London. He did not want the Irish members to be turned out of the House of Commons for the reason firstly, that he did not want England to be put into the'

===== 0002601_18930225_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
position of the superior. Superior in wealth, superior in power, superior in many ways she undoubtedly was; but just as he wanted every individual man to be equal before the law, so he wanted, the four nations also to be equal before the law. (Applause.) He should hate to vote for a provision in Parlia- ment which declared that Ireland was the sub- ject nation, and that England was the soverign naion, whereas God had made them to be one and equal bro- thers. (Applause.) He had another and very good reason for wanting the Irish members to be kept in the Parliament at Westminster. How on earth were they to

===== 0002601_18930225_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
GET RID OF THE LORDS

if they turned the Irish members out ? (Laughter.) That was one of the things he wanted to do, and one of the things he believed the voters in that constituency would thoroughly back him in trying to do. He wanted to get rid, once and for all, of the foolish system which enacted that because a man was Dorn he was to make laws for the people. The phrase

HOME RULE ALL ROUND reminded him that the previous night the idea was brought forward in a tangible form for the first time in tbe speech of one whom they used to consider a friend, namely, the Right Hon. Joseph Chamberlain. (Laughter groans, and a Voice: " Rat.") He mentioned the idea ' for the first time, and it was one which was extremely attractive to him (Mr. Brunner), and one which he hoped they would consider seriously and favourably henceforward. To his mind, the most business-like and the happiest solution of that treat and thorny question would be that not only should all Irish local affairs be settled in Ireland,but that all Scotch local affairs settled in Scotland, all Welsh local affairs settled iu Wales, and all English local affairs settled in England ; that each set of people should deal with that business which they understood thoroughly,and which others did not under- stand. There was one other detail in the Rome Rule Bill, besides the retention of members in the souse of Commons,which would hereafter excite a good deal of discussion. He referred to

THE ARRANGEMENT OF FINANCE.

England was an extremely rich count;;;;ltile Ireland was, perhaps, the poorest of all civilized countries. He believed shat it would be the desire of the Northwich division that Ireland should be able to raise the material condition of her people until it more nearly approached ' that of the condition of. the peopie of the rest of Great Britain, and if the Irish members could show that the financial proposals of the Home Rule Bill were unfair and harsh towards the poor country and niggardly in the hands of the rich people, he (the speaker) believed he would have the heartiest approval of his constituents when he voted in favour of a pro- posal to make, not only a fair, but a generous ar- rangement if the case need be. (Applause.) Again he must say that he accepted their ' congratula- tions, and thanked them for returning him to the House of Commons in that it had given him an opportunity of witnessing a scene in the House 48 or 50 hours ago,when at last the House ot Commons had condemned unani- mously, and almost without hesitation, the infamous methods with which the Tory party had pursued the Irish party in the House of Commons. (Applause.) There had been nothing too mean—no means too foul for the "Times" and the party of Pigott-.-(hisses)—to adopt in their pursuit of the Irish people. It had done him good to know that at last the confession was wrung out of the Tories that the Irish members were as honest as the others. (Applause.) The unhappy young man whose name had been mentioned, who made the foolish speech after dinner at the Liberal Unionist Club last Tuesday; spoke not only of the Gladstonian party as _

benefiters of the Irish party in that matter ; but of rich English partisans. He (Mr. Brunner) was not ,

ashamerrto confess before th4M that he had offered money to the Irish members of the House of Commons.

To hi; mind there was nothing honourable that he ought not to do seeing the way iv/which those men were pur- sued, nothing that would go against his honour that he ought not to do to help them in their trouble. He had offered them money publicly, and they had publicly re- fused it; he had offered money privately to them and privately they had refused it. (Applause.) He admired them before ; but since that he had held them in high honour. (Applause.) He had had the opportunity of witnessing a scene which he ventured to say would be historical as long as the English language was spoken. (Applause) It was a marvellous scene, when the

===== 0002601_18930225_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
OLD 'MAN, ELOQUENT,

master of his subject, master of his audience, spoke to the most brilliant collection of English statesmen and Englishmen that, he thought, ever were gathered to- gether to listen to a single voice. (Applause.) It was a scene which impressed.itself upon him, graven so deep that he did not think that as long as resson held its seat within him it would pass away and be forgotten. He could assure them as he uttered that touching perora- tion there was many a choking throat and many a dim eye in the assembly. (Applause.) . . .

Fletcher moved the following resolution : " That this meeting of Northwich Liberals cordially endorses the main principles of the Horns Rule Bill now before Parliament, and expresses satisfaction that the measure, whilst giving local self-government to Ireland, contains provisions strengthening the bonds of union between Great Britain and Ireland." Mr. W. Blagg seconded the motion which was carried by acclamation.

RUNCORN DISTRICT LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. The adjourned meeting to •further the formation of a district Liberal Association for Runcorn was held in the Union Hall,Regent-street,on Wednesday night. Mr. R. H. Rosnett occupied the chair, and there was a large attendance. At the last meeting it was resolved that a local Liberal Association be formed for the consideration of local and imperial questions. Mr. B. Kniveton moved and Mr. James Lea seconded a rider that the Association was to take action when deemed desirable. The motion was adopted. Rules for the guidance of the associa- tion were adopted and the subscription was fixed at is. per annum. The following officers were appoint- ed : President, Mr. R. H. Posnett ; vice-presidents, Mr. James Lea (North) ; Mr. A. R.Nornian (South); treasurer, Mr. Walter Millington ; secretaries, Mr. John Clark (North) ; Mr. A. Riley, (south) ; execu- tive committee ; Messrs. B. Kniveton, W. Carroll, P. Speakman, C.C. D. Bisbrown, Wm. Stanway. B. Crosby,Thos. Dean, Joseph Zoyle, Jao. Withington , Geo. Kirkham, S. Coventry. . HOME ROLE.

Mr. Kniveton moved " That this meeting of Runcorn Liberals hail with unbounded satisfaction the enthusias- tic reception accorded to the Irish Home Rule Bill, by the Great Liberal Party, and as evidenced by recent electoral victories, cordially approves of the principles of that measure' and expresses its heartiest wishes for the saccessful and speedy termination to the long and arduous struggle for Irish Constitutional rights." In en- forcing the points of the resolution, Mr. Kniveton con- trasted the position of the present Government with that ot the late Government at the commencement of their tenure of office, and said the Tories were not able to say aer.ss the floor of the House that the country was against the Government. (Applause). Mr. S. Coventry briefly seconded the motion. Mr. P. Speakman, in supporting the resolution, ex- pressed pleasure that under the Bill the Irish members were to be retained and referring to Mr. Gladstone's de- claration that he should not dissolve because the House of Lords rejected the Irish Government Bill, said he hoped Mr. Gladstone would adhere to his determination and continue in office to pass measures for the benefit of the three kingdoms. (Applause.) The speaker heartily welcomed the Registration Bill introduced by Mr. Fow- ler, and concluded by saying that the past history of the Liberal party was indicative of the future great triumphs. (Applause.) Mr. A. R. Norman welcomed a settlement of the Irish question on many grounds. and for one reason that it would relieve the Parliament of the congested condition into which its work had sunk. (Hear, hear.) The Government was now stronger than at the commence- ment of taking office, but he advised that various petty schemes should not The pressed too ardently, for he was satisfied that the members of the Government Board redeem all their promises if sufficient time were allowed them. (Applause.) The members of the Government wera the best judges of order and precedence. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Boyle contended that under Home Rule, Irishmen would use their privileges sensibly and would not oppress any section of their fellow. countrymen. The Chairman in a short speech said he was highly pleased with one feature of the Horne Rule Bill and that was the abolition of the Irish constabulary of whom he related some personal experiences. He urged that the Government should go to the House of Lords with mea- sures suitable to the wishes of the country, and they would simply be irresistible. (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimously. RUNCORN WATER BILL Mr. Boyle moved "That this meeting is of opinion that the ratepaying public are entitled to have informa- tion to enable them to form a correct estimate of the value of the Runcorn Water Company's concern,before be- ing asked for definite authority to purchase the same; the meeting therefore instructs the secretaries to write to the Board of Commissioners asking for copies of all informa- tion and reports relating to the same.—Mr. Roberts seconded.—Mr. Speakman thought it was not desirable that they should press for this information.—Mr. Hum- phreys corroborated and other ratepayers spoke in a similar strain. The resolution was then amended by the addition of the words "a week before the proposed next town's meeting," and in this form the resolution was carried. The Chairman asked fora volunteer to lead the discus- sion at the next meeting. Mr. Speakman said in his opinion the subject that should come before that Association shosild be for the political education of those in their midst, on the groat question of the day. .As to local matters he understood it was not intended to take so prominent a part except in cases where they, as an Association, saw there was very great need. That night there was a meeting being held for the purpose of forming a Ratepayers Association. There was plenty of work for both Associations to do, and he hoped that the Ratepayers Association now in process of formation would not come to the premature I

end that so many of its predecessors had done. If ther as members of the Libetal Association and as ratepayers could by an amalgamation of ideas do any good to the Ratepayers Association,or they Co the Liberal Association on local questions, it would be for the benefit of the town; but he thought the primary object of their Assoc iation was to have political discussions which would be an education and do them good as a party. (Applause.) Mr. Humphreys volunteered to introduce a discussion on the Manchester Licensing Bill at the next meeting.

LOCAL BLECTIONS.

Mr. W. Handley drew attention to the fact that a bill had been introduced into the House of Commons to. amend the law relating to the voting for and the quali- fication of members and electors of local authorities. provided for a proper register of electors, abolished plural voting and introduced voting by ballot. Boards of Commissioners were not included in the bill, and he suggested that a resolution be passed asking Mr. Brunner to endeavour to have the bill apillied to Boards of Coin- missioners.—Mr. Kniveton moved a resolution to that effect.—Mr. James Lea seconded, and Mr. Bisbrown heartily supported the motion as did also Mr. Speakman who said he had been pained for many years past in see- ing the way in which voters were brought to the poll at Runcorn, the system had been a standing disgrace to them. If Mr. Brunner could induce the introduction of the Bill to make it apply to Boards of Commissioners it would be a grand stroke of policy.—The resolution was carried and the meeting concluded.

A SCENE ON THI SHIP CANAL AT LATCHFORD.

WATCHING A• " HOISTING 7 OPERATION. An interesting feature of the work connected, with the erection of the high level cantilever Bridge- over the Ship Canal at Latchford, took place on Tuesday morning. The bridge, which is being erected by a Derby firm of engineers, consists of a, combination of stone and iron. There are three arches to it—two minor ones built of stone, and the large centre arch, reaching across the cutting, consisting of iron girders. These girders have been constructed on trestle supports over the cutting. In height they are about 12 feet, and are 70 feet long. They each weigh 32 tons, and in construction have occupied a large number of mechanics for many weeks. Both girders were finished by last week end, and on Tuesday the " hoisting "operation commenced; that is tp say, the difficult task ot lifting the girders from the trestle work to their proper positions, was launched upon. The distance they had to be hoisted would be 80 feet, and the apparatus tor the purpose con- sisted of two lengths of strong chain, connected with " crabs" or hand windlasses on the top of each minor arch, being firmly attached to each end of the girder. Only one girder was hoisted at once, of course. It had taken almost a week to arrange all this " tackle" in a- manner sufficiently safe to bear the tremendous weight and strain which one of those girders would place upon - it. Providing there was no wind, it was agreed to ccmmence operations at nine o'clock on Tuesday morn- ing. The spot preseneed a very busy sight about half- past the hour. Despite the chillness of the atmosphere, a large number of people sauntered about the spot, awaiting the operation. About 20 or 30 workmen were - stationed at each "windlass," whilst on the trestle work engineers and superintendentet busied themselves in bestowing final tests upon the fastenings; for it meant serious mis- chief if any of the tackle proved treacherous—it pro- bably meant loss of life besides loss of work. For about half-an-hour the air just above those girders was one thick myriad of fragmentary "tackle,"-.--pieces of chain, bolts, hammers, &c., some being lowered to those on the girders and some being hoisted to the workmen en the of the stone arches; there were hammers clanging on final work, and above their noise could be heard the deeP- hoarse voice of the superintendent of the work, shouting his orders through the clang and noise to the workraeW above.

At length the "tackle" was perfect. Every man took his stand at a captstan bar at the widdlass ; there was pause—quiet through the cessation of all noises, and the', the superintendent shouted "Hi there! Are you al-er right at that windlass." The reply came down "Aye aye, sir." Then he turned his head in the other direc- tion and shouted " There ! are you al-er-right at that 1 crab,'" "Aye, aye, sir." Another pause, and then 'stand fast—let her go—there steady!" The large A iron girder had begun to ascend, slowly rising, like a gigantic beam, into the air, where it remained for more than an hour, gradually going higher and higher, by the help of the " crabs,"until at length h reached its destiny' safely. It was considered one of the "tallest jobs" that's been on hand for some time. The other girder will not be hoisted for about a week. There are close upon 1,000 tans of iron used in the structure, which will forty one of the largest bridges in the neighbourhood, and will be the only one of its class on the Canal.

THZ NEW DEVIATION RAILWAYS.

We understand that it is the intention of the London and North-Western Railway Company to commence run' ning goods trains on the new deviation railways con- structed by the Manchester Ship Canal Company, earl,' next week. They will run as far as Moore in one direc- tion, and Thelwall in the other. Goods traffic will be conveyed on these lines for sit mouths before passenger trains will be allowed to run.

WHEREAS it has been represented to the Education Depart- ment that there is in part cf the School District of NEWTON-IN-MACKERFIELD that is to sty, in the Districts of EARLESTOWN and WA r GRAVE, an insuffici- ent r mount of Public School accommodation without payment of fees, for children over 3 and under 15 years of age, for whom such accommodation is desired; AND WHEREAS the Educational Department are satiSfied, after inquiry, that such is the case ; NOW THEREFORE, the Lords of the Committee of Council on Education have decided. and HEREBY GIVE NOTICE : I. The School district is the Parish of NEWTON-IN- MACKERFIELD. The Schools named in Schedule I. of this Notice supply Public School accommodation, without payment of fees, available for the said part of such District, for the number of Scholars mentioned in such Schedule. 111. Additional Public School accomodation without payment of fees, of the amount and description mentioned in Schedule 11. of this Notice, appears te be required for the said part of such District. RUNCORN IMPROVEMENT COMMISSIONERS RUNCORN WATER BILL. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that at a PUBLIC MEET- ING of the owners and ratepayers of the Distaict of the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners held in Bethesda Lecture Hall, Runcorn, on the lith day a February, 1893, in pursuance of the Act of the 35th and 36th Victoria. chapter 91 intituled "An Act to authorise the application ef funds of municipal Corporations and other governing bodies in certain macs," I as Chairman of such meeting, and in compliance with the Statute, proposed the following resolution Ti at this meeting as representinv the owners and ratepayers of the District of the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners do pursuant to the previsions of the Act of the 35th and 36th Victoria, chapter 91 intitule "An Act to authorise the applies, ion of funds of Hunicipa' Corporations and other governing bodies in certain cases" approve of and consent to the following re elutions of the Runcorn Improvement Corn- missii ners passed at their meeting held in the Town Hall, Runcorn, on the 28th day of November' 1892, namely t That application be made to Parliament in the Session of 1893 for a Bill to provide for the purchase by the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners, compulsorily or by agree- ment, of the undertaking of the Runcorn, Weston and Halton Waterworke Company, the winding up and dissolution of the Comoatty, the extension of powers for the Local Government of the District, the amendment of Acts, and for other pur- poses. That the costs, charges and expenses preliminary and Incidental to the applying for, promoting and passing such Bill into an Act shall be charged upon the Gener.,l District Rate," And that on being put to the meeting the said resolution was carried. Now I hereby declare the said resohdion to be duly passed and imlopted and hereby as required by law publish a copy of the same Dated this 17th day of February, 1891 FRANCIS WISWALL. Chairman of the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners.

Winces, L 4,010 -- -9-57 I 82 --- SOUTH MEATH. The result of the polling for the South Meath division was declared at Trim shortly after one o'clock on Satur- day. The figures were : Mr. Jeremiah Jordan (Nationalist) 2,707 Mr. J. J. Dalton (Redmondite) --- Majority 69 Last July Mr. Daltoa was defeated by Mr. Fullam, the figures being : Mr. P. Fullam (M'Cathyite) 2,122 Mr. J. J. Dalton (Parnellite) -- Majority B3 STOCKPORT. This election took place on Wednesday, and the result was declared as follows . G. Whiteley (C) M. A. Sharp-Hume (L) --- Majority 465 At the general election the figures were as follow : J. Leigh (L) L. J. Jennings (C) M. A. Sharp-Hume (L) C. P. Bowes-Lyon (C) 4,681 After the declaration of the poll, Mr. Joseph Leigh, M.P. for the borough, said in going about the town that day he had seen some sealed envelopes. He did not actually know what they contained, but he felt there was a condition of things existing in the borough which it would be their work as Liberals to alter. -- NORTH MEATH. Mr. James Gibney (Nationalist) was on Wednesday elected for North Meath in opposition to Mr. Pierce Mahony (Parnellite). The figures were:— Mr. J. Gibney (Nationalist) Mr. P. Mahony (Parnellite) --- Majority 258 At the general election the figures were : Mr. Davitt (Nationalist) 2,549 Mr. Mahony (Parnellite) 2,146 Mr. Gibney, addressing a meeting afterwards, said that the result of the election was the death blow of faction. He had triumphed despite the Tory vote, the men of that party having come from the uttermost ends of the country to oppose him. Mr. Mahony said the struggle would be continued, and he severely condemned the Roman Catholic clergy for the part they had taken in the contest. He ueclared that they had stooped to unworthy practice:, and had injured their own Church. -- Mr. Henri Josse, the Liberal member for Grimsby, has applied for the Chiltern Hundreds. His resignation has caused much regret among his cosistituents.

• ELOPEMENT AND DIVORCE. ----- OTTAWA, ONTARIO, THIIRSDAY.—The Senate Commit- tee on the Laws of Divorce have made a favourable re- port on the petition of Robert Young Holden for •a divorce from his wife, who eloped. with Mr. John Allan, of the Allan Steamship Line, some time ago. The cou- pls are said to be in Japan. • RAILWAY STRIKE IN AMERICA. WEDNESDAT.—AII the switchmen employed in the yards of the Chicago and Western Indiana Bell Railroad Company struck work to-night for an advance in wages. No trains can be moved either in or out of the Polk-street depot, and the yard traffic of the Grand Trunk, Santa Fe, and somo other roads are stopped. The depot and yards are being guarded by Pinkerton detectives. The railway employes. here now on strike number 300, and it is expected that the movement will spread to other roads. THE LIBERATOR SOCIETY. SUICIDE OF ONE OF THE VICTIMS. A man named William Caseley, a valet, committed ' suicide at Brighton on Thursday morning by blowingl out his brains with a revolver. A written statement was found on the deceased to the effect that he had in- vested money in the Liberator Building Society, to the failure of which is attributed the deed. The three defendants, named respectively Hobbs, Wright, and Newman, who are charged with several very serious frauds in connection with the affairs of the Liberator Building Society, were on Thursday com- mitted fer trial at the Central Criminal Court. The magistrate offered to accept bail for Newman to the amount of £5,000. MYSTERIOUS DEATH AT BLACKBURN. __- SUPPOSED MURDER. A terrible discovery was made on Wednesday after noon at Blackburn. A chemist named Walter Neilson was found by his assistant lying dead across the hearth- stone in the back room, with a bullet wound in his fore- head. He was lying on his back in a pool of blood. The assistant, shortly belore, was sent on an errand, leaving Mr, Nielson alone in the shop. On his return he found the door locked, but on a third attempt to get in—the door was open. Up to seven o'clock on Wednesday evening the police had failed to discover anything which elucidated the mystery surrounding the death of Mr. Neilson. Though the wound is evidently from a revol- ver, noweapon has been found, and there is a strong suspicion that Neilson was murdered, though nobody saw suspicious characters about, and apparently nothing in the shop has been disturbed. Neilson's watch was found upon him, and there was a little money in his pocket. If the tragedy should turn out to be a crime, the murderer must have gone into the shop, locked the door behind him' shot his victim in the back room, and left by the front door unobserved. OPENING OF THE INQUEST. The inquest on the body of Neilson was opened at Copy Nook Police Station on Thursday morning by Coroner Robinson, and after formal evidence of identification was adjourned. The chief constable states that so far the police are utterly baffled, there not being the slight- est clue in favour of the murder, suicide, or accident theories. Deceased had been heard to speak of suicide, and it is just possible he had destroyed himself, but the failure to find the weavon discounted this suggestion.
